⏺ I've read every file in the repo. Nothing is changed yet. Here's what I found,
  the design questions that matter most, and a suggested order of work.

  What the code tells us (beyond the README)

  The instrument (instrument.go) is the most important file. Several details in
  it drive the design:

  1. The ledger is written before any physical work starts, it survives 
     restarts, and the driver can read it through inst.Status(). That gives the
     driver a durable record of what physically happened, and we don't have to
     build one.
  2. JobID is ours to choose, and the ledger records it. If we use a predictable
     ID like {run}/{step}/{attempt}, a driver that receives the same command
     twice can look in Status() and see it already ran. It can then report the
     earlier result instead of running the step again. I think this is the key
     to check 5 (no step runs twice) when messages are delivered more than once.
  3. Faults are rolled from (seed, device, step, attempt), and attempt counts 
     earlier ledger entries for that run and step. So a retry gets a new roll.
     Retries are meaningful and repeatable for a given seed.
  4. A hung job never returns. Cancelling ctx only stops our waiting. The device
     stays busy until HANG_DURATION, and then the ledger says hung but nobody
     is told. So someone needs a timeout, and has to wait for the device to free
     up before a retry.
  5. The ledger keeps start/finish times but not the plate reader's data, only 
     its SHA-256. If the driver loses the Data bytes before storing them, they
     are gone. measure() is private, so we can't regenerate them.
  6. ErrBusy means "nothing ran", so it is always safe to retry.
  7. If the driver restarts mid-job, the ledger marks that job hung. The chaos
     tools never kill a driver, but restart: on-failure can restart one.

  Executor side:
  - NextSteps only guards devices within one run. Two runs at once would hit
    ErrBusy across runs.
  - NextSteps only treats running as occupying a device. If we add an internal
    state like "dispatched, not yet started", we have to map it to running
    before calling the scheduler.
  - StartRun doesn't check whether the update matched any row, so starting a run
    twice is silently accepted.
  - Checks 3 and 4 compare timestamps across steps that ran on different 
    machines. If we stamp started_at with the executor's receive time and
    finished_at with something else, we can create false dependency violations.
    I'd take both times from the instrument's ledger entry. That's also the
    honest answer to "as far as you know".

  Housekeeping: this directory isn't a git repo yet. Submission needs one, and
  committing as we go helps you explain the work later.

  The core design questions

  1. Commands, executor → driver. I'd use a JetStream work-queue stream with one
  durable consumer per device, e.g. cmd.<device>, with MaxAckPending=1. It
  survives an executor crash, and a driver that was disconnected picks commands
  up when it reconnects. It also lines up across runs for free, which avoids
  cross-run ErrBusy. The alternative, core request/reply with executor retries,
  is simpler but the executor then has to carry all the delivery guarantees
  itself.

  2. Events, driver → executor. A JetStream stream (evt.<device>) with one
  durable consumer on the executor. Each event is applied to Postgres in a
  transaction and acknowledged only after commit. Two hard parts:
  - The driver's outbox. While disconnected, a JetStream publish fails, so the
    driver has to keep the outcome and retry until it gets an ack.
  - When to ack the command. I'd ack only after the outcome event is
    acknowledged, and send InProgress() heartbeats during long jobs. If the
    driver dies, the command is redelivered, and the ledger check from point 2
    above makes that safe.

  3. Executor state and recovery. All state lives in Postgres. Each step gets an
  attempt and a job_id. To dispatch, we write the intent to the DB first, then
  publish with Nats-Msg-Id = job_id so JetStream drops duplicates. On startup,
  and on a periodic tick, a reconcile pass republishes anything dispatched but
  not finished and runs NextSteps on active runs. Every state change is guarded,
  e.g. UPDATE … WHERE status = 'running' AND job_id = $x, so a late or
  duplicate event can't move a step backwards.

  4. Hangs and timeouts. Who decides a job is hung: the driver, which knows the
  step duration, or the executor, using a lease or deadline? Then what does the
  record say:
  - Liquid handler: the step is failed ("instrument hung, outcome unknown"). It
    can't be safely retried.
  - Incubator and plate reader: wait until the device is free (Status().Busy == 
    false), then retry with attempt+1, up to a limit.

  5. Plate reader data. I lean towards the driver uploading to S3 under a fixed
  key (e.g. runs/{run}/{step}/{job_id}.csv). It retries while storage is down,
  then reports completed with the data_ref and SHA. The alternative is to send
  the ~1.6 KB of data over NATS and have the executor upload. That's workable,
  but it puts storage outages in the executor's path. Either way, the rule is:
  never report completed without a stored object.

  6. Cancel semantics. We can't stop physical work. My suggestion:
  - Pending steps become cancelled right away.
  - Running steps stay running until the instrument actually finishes, and are
    recorded with their real outcome.
  - The run becomes cancelled once nothing is in flight.
  - Commands already queued for a cancelled run have to be dropped. Options: the
    driver checks a NATS KV key for cancelled runs, or the executor only
    dispatches when the device is free so the queue stays empty.

  7. Failure policy. If a non-retryable failure happens on one branch, do steps
  still running on other branches finish? (They have to physically, so the
  record should show them.) Is the run failed only once everything has settled?

  GraphQL (after messaging is solid)

  The REST StepView hides retries: it shows one status per step. A scientist who
  wants the true record wants attempts: what physically ran, when, the outcome,
  and the data with its SHA-256.

  A possible shape is Run → Step → [Attempt], plus DataObject {ref, sha256, 
  size}, mutations createRun/startRun/cancelRun, and either polling or a
  runUpdated subscription for tools that follow runs.

  For the library, I'd pick gqlgen (schema-first, typed) or
  graph-gophers/graphql-go (lighter). Let's decide when we get there.

  Suggested order of work

  1. Baseline: git init, bring the stack up, watch acceptance.sh fail, run the
     examples to get a feel for NATS.
  2. Happy path on the final layout, with streams, consumers and job IDs, but no
     hardening. Get the 7 checks passing.
  3. Break it one fault at a time: kill the executor mid-run, disconnect each
     driver, take storage down, turn on hangs, turn on failures. Each time,
     write down the command and seed, what went wrong in the record, and the
     fix. That log becomes NOTES §4.
  4. Harden in response to each finding, rather than guessing up front.
  5. Cancel, retries and concurrent runs.
  6. GraphQL.
  7. NOTES.md, leaving a good hour for it.

  A small script that runs a workflow under chaos.sh random --seed N and then
  runs the acceptance checks would make step 3 repeatable. It would probably pay
  for itself.

  Decisions I'd like your view on before we start

  - Commands: JetStream per-device work queues (my preference), or core
    request/reply with executor-side retries?
  - Data upload: driver uploads (my preference), or executor uploads?
  - Concurrent runs: support several runs sharing devices, or declare one run at
    a time and note it as a boundary?
  - Retry budget and hang timeout for retryable devices, e.g. 3 attempts and a
    timeout of about 3× the step duration?
  - Pace: do you want to write the code yourself while I review and suggest, or
    have me implement each step in small increments for you to review? This
    matters because you'll need to walk them through your reasoning.

⏺ I read pstack's README and its main routing skill (poteto-mode) through the
  GitHub API. Here's a lighter process built on its core ideas.

  What to take from pstack, and what to leave out

  pstack has about 80 skills, but a handful of ideas carry most of the value for
  a three-hour challenge:

  ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          pstack idea           │         What it becomes for us         │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Sequence verifiable units.     │ One slice = one behaviour + the check  │
  │ Every unit ends in a check.    │ that proves it + one commit            │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Prove it works against the     │ The instrument's ledger is the truth.  │
  │ real thing, not a proxy        │ "It compiles" or "the API says         │
  │                                │ completed" is not proof.               │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Build the lever. Build the     │ One script that compares the run       │
  │ tool that proves the work.     │ record with the ledgers after any run, │
  │                                │  chaos or not                          │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Attack the premise / candour   │ I'm expected to push back, and "no,    │
  │ over agreement                 │ this doesn't earn its place" is an     │
  │                                │ acceptable answer                      │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Decision trail                 │ A short decisions log that becomes     │
  │ (show-me-your-work)            │ NOTES §2 almost unedited               │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Test behaviour, not            │ Tests assert what a scientist or the   │
  │ implementation                 │ ledger would see                       │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Make operations idempotent     │ The core of this challenge, so it goes │
  │                                │  in our review checklist               │
  └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Left out: multi-model panels, swarms and arenas, model routing, PR babysitting
  and shipping playbooks, and most of the principle files. They're built for
  multi-day programs and would cost more time than they save here.

  Proposed setup: one file of conventions, two logs, three skills, one script

  1. CLAUDE.md (about 40 lines). Project rules that load every session:
  - The invariant: the record must be true, and the ledger decides.
  - Fixed files and fixed shapes (instrument.go, RunView, the HTTP API).
  - The slice loop below.
  - A review checklist specific to this system. For every message: what if it
    arrives twice, late, never, or after a restart? Who acknowledges it, and
    when? Where do timestamps come from? Can a state change go backwards?
  - Working rules: you make the decisions, I propose and challenge. Every claim
    is labelled as measured, inferred or guessed.

  2. Two logs that turn into the note. Writing them as we go means NOTES.md is
  mostly assembly at the end, not a memory exercise.
  - docs/decisions.md: each entry records the decision, the alternatives, why,
    and the evidence. This feeds NOTES §1–3.
  - docs/chaos-log.md: each entry records the command or seed, what we
    predicted, what we saw, and the fix. This feeds NOTES §4–5.

  3. Three project skills in .claude/skills/. They're committed, so reviewers
  see the process alongside the AI conversations.

  - /slice <behaviour> is the main loop:
    a. State the behaviour in one sentence, and which test or check proves it.
    b. Write that test and watch it fail.
    c. Make the smallest change that passes it.
    d. Run the checks.
    e. Do a quick review against the CLAUDE.md checklist.
    f. Commit with a "why" message.
    g. Reply with three lines: what changed, why, and the evidence.
  - /decide <question> is for design forks:
    - Lay out 2–3 real alternatives.
    - Name the assumption each one depends on, and attack it.
    - Settle any factual question by experiment, not opinion.
    - Make a recommendation, then stop for your choice and record it in
      decisions.md.
    - This keeps you the owner of every decision you'll be asked to defend.
  - /break <fault> is a chaos experiment:
    a. Predict what will happen first.
    b. Run the fault against a live run.
    c. Run the truth checker.
    d. Log the result to chaos-log.md.
    e. If the record was false, the fix becomes the next /slice.
    - Predicting first is what makes this an experiment rather than poking at
      the system.

  For review, use the built-in /code-review at the end of each milestone, not a
  custom skill. It will read the CLAUDE.md checklist anyway.

  4. The lever: scripts/check-truth.sh <run-id>. acceptance.sh only checks the
  happy path, and it fails on legitimate outcomes like a step that failed
  honestly. The truth checker asserts invariants that must hold whatever 
  happened:
  - Every completed step has a completed ledger execution.
  - Every failed step has a matching failed or hung execution, or an explicit
    reason.
  - Every data_ref points to an object whose hash matches the ledger.
  - No execution happened that the record doesn't account for.
  - Nothing ran after a cancel that the record doesn't show.

  I think this is the most valuable piece of the setup.

  Testing levels

  Level: Unit (go test)
  What it covers: Step state machine, applying events idempotently, mapping
  internal states to the scheduler
  Speed: Milliseconds. TDD lives here.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Level: Driver integration
  What it covers: Serve against a real instrument.New (temp ledger, 50 ms steps)

  and an in-process NATS server with JetStream. Can test duplicate commands,
  redelivery and dropped connections without Docker.
  Speed: Seconds
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Level: System
  What it covers: Docker stack + acceptance.sh + check-truth.sh + chaos
  Speed: Minutes. One run at each milestone.

  The in-process NATS server means adding nats-server/v2 as a test-only
  dependency. It's worth it: it turns delivery guarantees from something we
  argue about into something we test.

  Git conventions

  Keep it light:
  - Work on dev, one commit per slice.
  - Commit messages say why, not just what.
  - At the end, open one PR from dev to main whose description summarises the
    milestones.
  - Export the AI conversations to ai/ as we go.
  - Write session start and end times at the top of decisions.md, so "how long
    you spent" is a fact, not a guess.

  Where I'd push back on myself

  Three skills might still be one too many. /break could be a section of /slice.
  I'd keep it separate because predicting before running is a different mindset
  from building, and it's the step most likely to be skipped under time
  pressure. If the setup itself takes more than about 20 minutes, it isn't
  lightweight any more and we should cut it back.

  Decisions for you

  1. Skills: all three (/slice, /decide, /break), or fold /break into /slice?
  2. Log location: docs/decisions.md and docs/chaos-log.md, or one JOURNAL.md?
  3. Test dependency: OK to add the in-process NATS server for driver tests?
  4. Who writes code: I implement each slice for you to review, or you write and
     I review? This changes how the /slice skill is worded.

  Once you've answered, I'll write CLAUDE.md, the three skills and the empty
  logs as the first commit on dev.

  Sources:
  - michael-denyer/pstack-claude (GitHub), README and
    plugins/pstack/skills/poteto-mode/SKILL.md, read through gh api
  - pstack-claude on DeepWiki (found by search, but the fetch was rate-limited,
    so not read)
  - explainx.ai overview of pstack

  Created PR #1 (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The process is set up and open for your review as PR #1 into dev:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/1. No application
  code has been touched.

  What's in the PR:
  - CLAUDE.md loads every session. It holds the invariant (the record must be
    true, and the ledger decides), the fixed files and shapes, our roles, the
    git flow, and a six-question review checklist. The checklist asks what
    happens if a message arrives twice, late or never, or if the process
    restarts mid-path. It also asks who acknowledges what and when, whether a
    status can move backwards, where timestamps come from, and whether the
    record could disagree with the ledger.
  - /slice <behaviour> works on a slice/<name> branch off dev:
    a. Frame the slice and wait for your OK.
    b. Write a failing check.
    c. Make the smallest change that passes it.
    d. Verify, then self-review against the checklist.
    e. Run codex review --base dev once, and sort each finding into fix, defer
       or reject.
    f. Explain it to you: how it works, why this way, what could go wrong, the
       evidence, the Codex findings, and a couple of questions you should be
       able to answer.
    g. Open the PR only after you agree.
    - Slices over about 150 lines of non-test code get split.
  - /decide <question> lays out 2–3 real alternatives, attacks the assumption
    each rests on, settles factual questions with short experiments, recommends
    one, and then stops for your choice. Your choice is recorded in
    docs/decisions.md.
  - /break <fault>: predict first, then inject the fault, then compare the
    record with the ledgers and the S3 hashes. It's logged to docs/chaos-log.md,
    and any fix becomes a new /slice rather than happening inside /break.
  - Logs: docs/decisions.md, with a time log for the "how long" answer, and
    docs/chaos-log.md, both with templates. There's also an empty ai/ folder for
    exported conversations.

  Choices I made that you might want to change:
  - PRs go into dev, with one final PR from dev to main. The alternative is for
    slices to go straight to main and drop dev, which is simpler. I kept dev
    because you created it.
  - I didn't run Codex on this PR. It's process documents, so the first slice
    will be the real test of that step.
  - The truth checker is deferred to its own slice, since it's tooling rather
    than process.
  - New skills may need a session restart before /slice and the others show up.
    I haven't checked that they appear yet.

  Once you've reviewed and merged PR #1, I'd suggest starting with a short
  /decide on the command and event topology, since the first slice depends on
  it.

  1. The skills load, but the Codex step was broken.
  - /slice, /decide and /break now show up in my skill list (they need a fresh
    session), so they're discoverable.
  - When I ran the review step for real, it failed: codex review --base rejects
    custom instructions ('--base <BRANCH>' cannot be used with '[PROMPT]'). The
    first slice would have hit that error.
  - I replaced it with scripts/codex-review.sh. It runs codex exec in read-only
    mode against the diff from dev, with our review instructions, and prints
    only Codex's verdict.
  - I ran it on this PR twice. It worked and found nothing, which is expected
    for a docs-only diff.

  2. You were right: the principle overstated what the ledger proves. From
  reading instrument.go:
  - On the plate reader, ledger completed doesn't mean the data is safe. The
    bytes exist only in memory, and the ledger stores just their SHA-256. A
    completed read whose object is missing from storage is not completed in the
    record.
  - hung means the outcome is unknown, not that the step failed. It also covers
    a driver restart in the middle of a job.
  - The ledger knows nothing about pending, cancelled or retryable. Those are
    the executor's own state.
  - The ledger itself can be wrong. If its end-of-job write fails, a restart
    reports that completed job as hung (line 241). That needs a disk error, so
    it's rare, but it's worth naming in the note.

  CLAUDE.md now states the invariant as: every claim needs its own evidence. The
  ledger backs physical claims. A stored object with a matching hash backs data
  claims. With no evidence, the record makes no claim: running while we can
  still find out, then failed with the reason. Review question 6 and the compare
  step in /break check this directly.

  This has a design consequence: a plate reader step only becomes completed
  after its data is stored. That will shape the upload decision in our first
  /decide.

  If PR #1 looks right to you, merge it and I'll start that /decide on the
  topology.

⏺ I'll follow /decide up to the recommendation, then stop for your choice.
  Nothing is written to the decisions log yet.

  1. The question

  How do commands reach the drivers, and how do outcomes get back to the 
  executor, so that the record stays true when messages are lost, duplicated or 
  late, and processes or networks drop?

  This is the main decision because every way the record can go wrong is a
  message that went missing, arrived twice, or arrived after we'd already acted
  on a guess.

  2. What correctness needs, whichever transport we pick

  This is the most important point. No NATS mode gives exactly-once delivery, so
  correctness has to come from four things the transport can't provide:

  1. Durable intent before any message is sent. The executor writes job_id and
     attempt to Postgres first, then sends. After a crash, it can tell what it
     may have sent.
  2. A predictable JobID such as run-abc/combine/1. When a command arrives, the
     driver looks in inst.Status(): is this job current, or already in the
     ledger? If so, it reports that job instead of running it again. This works
     because the ledger is written before the work starts and survives restarts
     (instrument.go:166-170, 311). It's what stops a duplicate message from
     becoming a second physical run (acceptance check 5).
  3. Guarded state changes. For example, UPDATE steps … WHERE job_id = $1 AND 
     status = 'running'. A late or duplicate result can't move a step backwards
     or overwrite a newer attempt.
  4. A way to find out the truth again: ask the driver about the job (it answers
     from the ledger), or check storage for the object. This is what recovers
     from an executor crash, and nothing else does.

  Plate reader data adds one more piece. If the object key comes from the job ID
  (runs/<run>/<step>/<job_id>.csv), the executor can check whether the data is
  stored and compare its hash with the ledger. The driver doesn't have to
  remember anything.

  So the transport choice decides how much buffering and retrying we write 
  ourselves. It doesn't decide whether the system is correct.

  3. How a job moves through the system (same for every option)

  executor (Postgres)                         driver                     
  instrument
  ───────────────────                         ──────                     
  ──────────
  step pending
    │ NextSteps says go
    ▼
  write job_id, attempt=1  ── "run job_id" ──▶ already in ledger?
  status: dispatched                            no ─▶ inst.Run(job) ────▶ 
  ledger: running
    │                                                    (2s of work)
    │                                                                     
  ledger: completed
    │                                                                     (+ 
  sha256 if reader)
    │                                          got Outcome
    │                                          plate reader: PUT data to S3
    │                                          (retry until stored)
    │◀── "job_id: completed, times, ref" ─────
  guarded UPDATE → completed
  NextSteps → dispatch the next steps

  The two-way exchange each option has to deliver:
  - executor → driver: "run this job".
  - driver → executor: "here is what happened to this job".

  4. The alternatives

  A. Core request/reply, and the executor asks for the truth

  - Subjects:
    - lab.<device>.run {job_id, run_id, step} → the driver replies at once with
      accepted, busy or known, then runs the job in the background.
    - lab.<device>.job {job_id} → the driver answers from Status() and its
      upload state: running, completed (with times and ref), failed (with the
      retryable flag), or hung.
  - The executor runs one loop every ~500 ms. For each in-flight step, it asks
    about the job and applies the answer with a guarded update. For each run
    that can move, it calls NextSteps and dispatches.
  - Optional: the driver also publishes a core-NATS "job changed" nudge, so the
    executor asks straight away. If a nudge is lost, nothing breaks.
  - Makes easy:
    - Recovery is the normal path. The executor works out state by asking, every
      tick, so recovering from a crash is just the next tick. That path gets
      exercised on every run, not only in rare failures.
    - There's no stream state to reason about.
    - No commands sit in a queue, so cancel just means "stop dispatching".
    - A busy device replies busy, which means nothing ran, so the step simply
      stays pending.
  - Makes hard:
    - Results arrive with up to one tick of delay. The record's times come from
      the ledger, so accuracy isn't affected.
    - If several runs share a device, the executor does the queueing.
    - It doesn't scale to thousands of devices. That isn't a concern here.
  - Rests on: "anything the executor needs to know, it can find out again by
    asking." Testing that:
    - True for physical outcomes: the ledger is durable.
    - True for data: a predictable key plus the ledger's SHA-256.
    - Not true for Data bytes that were never uploaded. If the driver restarts
      between Run returning and the upload finishing, the bytes are gone. That's
      equally true for B and C, and the honest record is a failed attempt
      ("data lost"), retried because a read is retryable.
    - The chaos tools never kill a driver, but restart: on-failure can restart
      one.

  B. JetStream both ways

  - Streams and consumers:
    - Stream CMDS, subjects cmd.<device>, work-queue retention, with one durable
      pull consumer per device (MaxAckPending=1).
    - Stream EVENTS, subjects evt.<device>, with one durable consumer on the
      executor.
  - Commands: the executor publishes with Nats-Msg-Id = job_id so JetStream
    drops duplicates.
  - The driver: fetch the command → check the ledger → run → publish started and
    then finished (retrying until JetStream acknowledges) → ack the command,
    sending InProgress() heartbeats while it works.
  - The executor: consumes events, applies them in a DB transaction, and acks
    after commit.
  - Makes easy:
    - Messages wait across executor outages and driver disconnects.
    - There's a natural per-device queue across runs, and an event history.
  - Makes hard: there are many more settings to get right, and each has a
    failure mode:
    - AckWait against step durations and 30 s hangs. A heartbeat lost during a
      disconnect leads to redelivery while the job is still running.
    - The dedupe window, which is 2 minutes by default.
    - Queued commands for a run that has since been cancelled.
    - A second place where state lives: the stream can say "pending command"
      while Postgres says "cancelled".
  - Rests on: "durable messaging makes delivery reliable, so the record stays
    true." Testing that: it's only partly true.
    - At-least-once delivery means duplicates. We still need point 2 above.
    - A crash between the DB write and the publish loses the command. We still
      need an outbox or a reconcile step.
    - A hang produces no event at all. We still need a deadline.
    - So JetStream adds buffering, not correctness, and we still have to build a
      recovery path that rarely runs.

  C. Hybrid: request/reply for commands, JetStream for events, plus a status 
  query

  - Commands are A's lab.<device>.run: synchronous, with no stale queue.
  - Results go into B's EVENTS stream, published with a Msg-Id and retried until
    acknowledged, and consumed durably by the executor.
  - A's lab.<device>.job query covers startup recovery and steps that have gone
    quiet.
  - Makes easy: buffering in the direction that matters most. The driver
    produces facts that must not be lost while the executor is down.
  - Makes hard: two mechanisms and two recovery paths. It still needs A's query
    for the gap between a DB write and a publish.
  - Rests on: "push events are needed for correctness or latency." Testing that:
    not for correctness, as points 2–4 show. For latency, a core nudge gets
    most of the way there.

  5. What happens when things fail

  Fault: Command lost, or its reply lost
  A (ask): Executor resends the same job_id. Driver says known.
  B (JetStream both ways): Stored or not. If the publish wasn't acked, the
  reconcile step resends.
  C (hybrid): Same as A
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Duplicate command
  A (ask): Ledger check → no rerun
  B (JetStream both ways): Msg-Id dedupe within 2 min, then the ledger check
  C (hybrid): Ledger check
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Executor killed mid-run
  A (ask): Next tick after restart asks about every in-flight job
  B (JetStream both ways): Events wait in the stream. A crash between the DB
  write and the publish needs reconcile.
  C (hybrid): Events wait. Startup query covers the gap.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Executor disconnected
  A (ask): Questions time out, steps stay running (no evidence, so no claim)
  B (JetStream both ways): Events buffer, then are consumed
  C (hybrid): Events buffer
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Driver disconnected mid-job
  A (ask): Instrument finishes. Questions time out. After reconnect the answer
  is
  completed.
  B (JetStream both ways): Driver retries the publish. AckWait may cause a
  redelivery, which the ledger check makes safe.
  C (hybrid): Driver retries the publish
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Storage down after a read
  A (ask): Driver retries the upload and answers "uploading". Record stays
  running.
  B (JetStream both ways): Driver delays the finished event
  C (hybrid): Same
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Instrument hangs
  A (ask): Driver answers running, the executor's deadline expires, then hang
  policy applies
  B (JetStream both ways): No event. Needs a deadline anyway.
  C (hybrid): Needs a deadline
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Run cancelled
  A (ask): Stop dispatching. Nothing is queued.
  B (JetStream both ways): Queued commands still get delivered and must be
  dropped
  C (hybrid): Nothing queued

  6. Facts: measured, inferred and guessed

  - Inferred from the code:
    - The ledger records JobID before the work starts and survives restarts.
    - Status() exposes the current job and every execution.
    - Plate reader bytes exist only in the Outcome.
    - Both processes reconnect to NATS forever (MaxReconnects(-1) in both
      main.go files).
  - Guess, worth measuring: what a chaos.sh disconnect does to an open NATS
    connection.
    - Docker removes the network interface, but the TCP connection may survive a
      2–7 s gap, with retransmits delivering the delayed bytes.
    - Or the client may only notice through pings. nats.go's default is 2 min ×
      2 missed pings, so a dead connection could go unnoticed for minutes, and
      core publishes in that window are silently lost.
    - This matters a lot for B and C (stuck heartbeats, publish timeouts), and
      for how we set request timeouts in A.
    - Proposed experiment (~10 min, throwaway): a small Go program publishes a
      numbered message on core NATS every 200 ms and sends a request every
      second, while I run chaos.sh disconnect on its container. We record which
      messages arrive, how long requests take to fail, and when the client
      reports the disconnect.
    - A is the option whose correctness doesn't depend on the answer. That's
      part of why I recommend it.

  7. Recommendation: A, plus a lossy core nudge to cut latency

  The executor's DB loop asks the drivers, and the drivers answer from the
  ledger and storage. Every guarantee rests on things that are durable on their
  own (Postgres, the ledger, storage) rather than on messages getting through.
  The recovery path is the normal path, so every acceptance run tests it. It's
  also the smallest system you'd have to explain.

  The honest cost: it uses no JetStream, even though the README "switches it
  on". We'd defend that in the note: JetStream buys buffering, and this design
  doesn't need buffering to be correct.

  What would change my mind:
  - If the disconnect experiment shows requests hanging for minutes, polling
    would stall badly. That's fixable with ping settings, though.
  - If you want steps from several runs to queue across devices. B's per-device
    work queue gives that for free.
  - If you see the event history in the stream as valuable for the record
    itself.

  Your choice: A (my recommendation), B, C, or something else? Do you also want
  me to run the ~10-minute disconnect experiment before you decide? It would
  turn the biggest guess here into a measurement either way.

⏺ The page is open in your browser:
  /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-yudu-Dev-Automata-distributed-systems-interview
  /0cedd254-1b86-400e-ab6b-c50aa779e92c/scratchpad/d1-messaging.html

  It goes in this order:
  1. The question.
  2. The four correctness cards, with the takeaway that the transport decides
     buffering, not correctness.
  3. A four-lane diagram of a job's life: executor, NATS, driver, instrument and
     ledger.

✻ Sautéed for 13s · done Monday 00:29

※ recap: We're designing the lab automation challenge, starting with how the executor and drivers 
  talk over NATS. Next, you choose option A, B or C, and say whether I should first run the 
  10-minute disconnect experiment. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ Use decide skill to explore the messaging architecture between the executor   
  and drivers.                                                                  
  Consider long-term production reliability, scalability, maintainability, and  
  operational complexity, rather than optimizing mainly for simplicity or       
  implementation speed.                                                         
  Compare the possible approaches, challenge their assumptions, and recommend a 
  design that balances production-quality engineering with the scope of this    
  challenge.                                                                    
  Let's discuss the trade-offs and no app implementation yet.                   

⏺ Skill(decide)
  ⎿  Successfully loaded skill

⏺ 1. The question

  Which messaging design between the executor and the drivers would we be happy 
  to run in production, with more devices, more runs, more than one executor 
  replica and real on-call staff, while keeping the record true and staying 
  buildable within this challenge?

  The previous pass favoured simplicity. Weighted towards production, my
  recommendation changes. The facts haven't changed. What changed is how much
  weight each one gets, and I'll say where.

  2. What doesn't change

  The four rules from last time hold for every option: write intent before
  sending, a predictable JobID checked against the ledger, guarded state
  changes, and a way to find the truth again. A production lens adds four more
  requirements:

  ┌─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       Requirement       │         Why it matters in production          │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Fail safe when acting   │ A command that runs minutes late, after a     │
  │ on equipment. When in   │ cancel or after a plate was moved by hand, is │
  │ doubt, don't move.      │  worse than one that never runs               │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Facts must not depend   │ The executor gets deployed, crashes, gets     │
  │ on the executor being   │ scaled. Results produced meanwhile must       │
  │ up                      │ persist somewhere other than driver memory    │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                         │ Here the ledger is complete and can be        │
  │ Don't depend on the     │ queried. Real vendor APIs often aren't.       │
  │ instrument's history    │ Recovery should work from our own records,    │
  │                         │ with the instrument as a cross-check          │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                         │ Lag, redeliveries, stuck jobs and dead        │
  │ Ops can see it          │ devices should show up in metrics, not only   │
  │                         │ in logs                                       │
  └─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  3. The alternatives, through a production lens

  A. Request/reply plus executor polling

  This is the earlier recommendation.

  - Production strengths:
    - Fail safe: no hidden queue, so nothing runs late.
    - Backpressure is explicit: a busy driver replies busy.
    - The recovery path is the main path.
  - Production weaknesses:
    a. Results depend on executor uptime and driver memory. During a 10-minute
       executor outage, outcomes sit in driver RAM. A driver restart in that
       window throws them away, leaving only the ledger, which a real instrument
       may not keep.
    b. It doesn't scale out. Polling cost is bounded by the number of devices,
       since each device runs one job at a time, so load is fine up to hundreds
       of devices. But every tick costs a round trip per in-flight job, and with
       several executor replicas you need run ownership (leases) before anyone
       polls.
    c. No history. Nothing records "what the driver said, and when", apart from
       what we choose to write.
  - Rests on: "we can always ask the instrument." That's true here because the
    ledger is complete (instrument.go:311). It's a property of this simulator,
    not of lab equipment in general. This is the assumption that breaks in 
    production.

  B. JetStream both ways

  - Production strengths:
    - Commands and events persist through any outage.
    - Executor replicas can share a durable consumer.
    - Consumer lag and redelivery counts are built-in metrics.
    - Clean decoupling.
  - Production weaknesses:
    a. A durable command queue is the opposite of fail safe. A driver that was
       disconnected for 5 minutes reconnects and physically runs a command the
       executor has since given up on, or a cancelled run's step. Adding
       expires_at to commands and re-checking with the executor before running
       fixes this, but then the queue is no longer the source of truth.
    b. A FIFO queue does the scheduling. Priorities, fairness across runs, and
       pulling one run's queued steps after a cancel are all hard in a
       work-queue stream. The executor is the right place for scheduling policy,
       and once it is, a command queue deeper than 1 adds nothing.
    c. Tuning AckWait and heartbeats for physical jobs. Durations vary from 2 s
       to hours (an incubation), and hangs last 30 s. Every mismatch means a
       redelivery of a command that is still running.
    d. Two places hold job state: the stream's unacked commands and Postgres.
  - Rests on: "durability is always good." It's good for facts, which must not
    be lost. It's risky for intentions: an intention that persists can become
    stale.

  C. Commands by request/reply, events through JetStream, plus a reconcile query

  - Commands: core request to lab.<device>.cmd.run. The driver replies accepted,
    busy or known synchronously. The executor keeps the queue in Postgres and
    only sends to a device it believes is free.
  - Events: the driver publishes accepted, started and finished to the JetStream
    stream LAB_EVENTS, with Nats-Msg-Id set to job ID plus event kind. It
    retries until JetStream acknowledges. The executor reads them through a
    durable pull consumer, applies each idempotently, and acks after the DB
    commit.
  - Reconcile query: lab.<device>.job request/reply, used on executor startup
    and for in-flight steps that have been quiet past their deadline.
  - Production strengths:
    - Facts are durable and replayable; intentions are fail safe.
    - Executor replicas can share the event consumer, because guarded updates
      make concurrent apply safe.
    - Stream lag is a metric ops can watch.
    - The event history gives a 7-day audit trail of what the drivers said.
    - It doesn't depend on the instrument's history, because the stream is our
      own record. The ledger becomes a cross-check.
  - Production weaknesses:
    - Two mechanisms to explain and operate.
    - The driver still holds an event in memory until JetStream acknowledges it.
      A small window, but real.
    - Streams have to be provisioned (retention, storage limits, replicas).
  - Rests on: "the line between facts and intentions is the right one to design
    around." Testing that:
    - Physical facts can't be undone. Losing them is exactly how the record
      becomes false, so they should be durable.
    - Physical actions can't be undone either, so a stale one is dangerous and
      they should be short-lived.
    - I think this holds for any lab system, not just this simulator.

  D. Shared state in a NATS KV store

  Briefly, so we can rule it out deliberately: drivers write jobs.<job_id> into
  NATS KV and the executor watches it. This makes KV a second source of truth
  beside Postgres, with no transactions across the two. Rejected.

  4. Side-by-side

  Facts survive executor outage + driver restart
  A: polling: ✗ (ledger only)
  B: JS both ways: ✓
  C: hybrid: ✓
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  No stale physical commands
  A: polling: ✓
  B: JS both ways: ✗ without extra checks
  C: hybrid: ✓
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Scheduling policy (fairness, priority, cancel)
  A: polling: executor ✓
  B: JS both ways: stream FIFO ✗
  C: hybrid: executor ✓
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Several executor replicas
  A: polling: needs run leases
  B: JS both ways: ✓ shared consumer
  C: hybrid: ✓ shared consumer (scheduling still needs per-run locks)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ops visibility
  A: polling: logs only
  B: JS both ways: stream/consumer metrics
  C: hybrid: event stream metrics
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Audit trail
  A: polling: ✗
  B: JS both ways: ✓
  C: hybrid: ✓
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Doesn't rely on instrument history
  A: polling: ✗
  B: JS both ways: ✓
  C: hybrid: ✓
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Mechanisms to operate
  A: polling: 1
  B: JS both ways: 2 streams, AckWait tuning
  C: hybrid: 1 stream + request/reply
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Challenge scope
  A: polling: smallest
  B: JS both ways: largest
  C: hybrid: medium

  5. Design details that matter for C, whichever way you choose

  - One versioned message format: {v, job_id, run_id, step, attempt, device, 
    ts}. Subjects follow lab.<device>.cmd.*, lab.<device>.evt.* and
    lab.<device>.job, so one device can be watched or given permissions with a
    single wildcard.
  - The executor provisions the stream with CreateOrUpdateStream at startup,
    idempotently. Drivers never create infrastructure.
  - Ack after commit: an event is acknowledged only once Postgres has committed
    it. A crash before the ack means redelivery, and the guarded update makes
    that harmless.
  - Liveness is a deadline, not silence: each in-flight step has a deadline
    based on the step's duration. When it passes, the executor asks the
    reconcile query. Hang policy is a separate decision.
  - Device presence: a driver heartbeat (core publish, or a KV entry with a TTL)
    tells the scheduler which devices are reachable, and later the GraphQL API.
    This is optional and can be added in its own slice.
  - Deliberately out of scope:
    - multiple executor replicas (the design allows them, but we won't build
      per-run locking);
    - a durable driver-side outbox (the in-memory window until JetStream acks);
    - NATS clustering with 3 replicas.

    Each goes into the note as a known boundary.

  6. Facts: measured, inferred and guessed

  - Inferred: core request/reply fails fast when nobody is subscribed
    (ErrNoResponders, shown in examples/core_request_reply). A JetStream publish
    returns only once the server has stored the message
    (examples/jetstream_pub_sub).
  - Guess, and now more important: what chaos.sh disconnect does to an open
    connection. C depends on it twice:
    - does the driver's JetStream publish time out cleanly during a disconnect;
    - how long until the client notices a dead connection (default pings are 2
      min × 2), which affects when command requests start failing fast.

    The likely outcome is that we should set PingInterval to a few seconds.
    That's a guess until measured. I'd now treat the ~10-minute experiment as a 
    prerequisite, not an option.

  7. Recommendation: C, the hybrid

  The design rule: durable facts, short-lived intentions, Postgres decides.

  Why I've changed from A: A's main assumption, "we can always ask the
  instrument", is true of this simulator and false of most real equipment. Under
  a production lens that's decisive.

  Why not B: a durable command queue for physical actions trades a delivery
  problem for a safety problem (stale commands). It also moves scheduling into a
  FIFO queue, where it doesn't belong.

  Cost against the challenge: roughly one extra slice compared with A (stream
  setup, consumer and publish retry), plus explaining the reasoning in the note.
  I think that cost is worth it.

  What would change my mind:
  - If the disconnect experiment shows JetStream publishes behaving badly under
    partition, for example hanging well beyond their timeout. That would make
    the driver's retry loop the weak point.
  - If you want steps from many runs to queue for a device with the queue itself
    as the source of truth. That's B's territory, and it would need command
    expiry.
  - If we run short of time. A is a safe fallback, because C is A plus a durable
    event path, so we could build A first and add the stream.

  Your choice:
  1. C (recommended), A, B, or a variation?
  2. Shall I run the disconnect experiment now, before we record the decision?
     It's throwaway code in tmp/, not application code.

  Once you choose, I'll record the decision in docs/decisions.md. I can also
  update tmp/d1-messaging.html to this version if that helps.

⏺ The experiment is done: four runs, partitioning a NATS client exactly the way
  chaos.sh disconnect does. Here's what it showed, plus the driver-crash
  analysis. V2 of the page is being built from V1 in the background.

  What I ran (measured)

  - A probe container on lab-network sent a core publish every 200 ms, a request
    every 1 s (2 s timeout), and a JetStream publish every 1 s (5 s timeout,
    with a message ID).
  - A peer on the host was never partitioned. It counted the messages, answered
    the requests and owned the stream.
  - I cut the container off for 6 s and for 20 s, once with the drivers' current
    ping settings (nats.go defaults, 2 min) and once with 2 s pings.

  Run: default pings, 6 s
  Client noticed?: never (the TCP connection survived)
  Core msgs lost: 0 (late)
  Timed-out requests later delivered: not logged in this run
  JS publishes "failed" → actually stored: 1 → 1
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Run: default pings, 20 s
  Client noticed?: never
  Core msgs lost: 0 (late)
  Timed-out requests later delivered: 6 of 6, all at once about 20 s late
  JS publishes "failed" → actually stored: 3 → 3
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Run: 2 s pings, 6 s
  Client noticed?: "stale connection" after ~6 s, reconnected 1 s after the
  network came back
  Core msgs lost: 0
  Timed-out requests later delivered: 2 of 2
  JS publishes "failed" → actually stored: 1 → 1
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Run: 2 s pings, 20 s
  Client noticed?: stale after ~6 s, reconnected ~1 s after the network came
  back
  Core msgs lost: 30 lost
  Timed-out requests later delivered: 4 of 6 late, 2 lost
  JS publishes "failed" → actually stored: 3 → 2

  Findings

  F1. A timeout doesn't mean the message wasn't delivered. Requests the sender
  had given up on reached the other side up to 20 s later. In C, that means a
  "run job" command can reach a driver after the executor timed out, even after
  a cancel. This is the most important result. It changes C in three ways:
  - Commands carry an expires_at, and the driver refuses expired ones.
  - The executor treats a timeout as "unknown": it resends the same job_id,
    never starts a new attempt, and doesn't assume the device is free.
  - The design needed this even before the experiment. I had assumed timed-out
    requests were simply lost.

  F2. A JetStream publish that reports failure may have been stored. 7 of the 8
  "context deadline exceeded" publishes were in the stream. So the driver
  retries with the same Nats-Msg-Id, and the server drops the duplicate within
  its 2-minute window. The executor must still apply events idempotently,
  because a partition longer than the window can produce real duplicates.

  F3. The ping interval trades lateness for loss. With the drivers' current
  defaults, a 20 s partition was invisible: nothing was lost and everything
  arrived late. With 2 s pings, the client noticed in about 6 s, but whatever it
  had written into the dead connection was lost, and later messages were
  buffered and sent on reconnect. F1 and F2 cover both cases, so correctness
  doesn't depend on this setting. I'd still set it explicitly to a few seconds,
  so disconnects are noticed quickly and show up in logs.

  Driver crashes after the physical work, before the event is published

  From instrument.go: the ledger is updated to completed before Run returns the
  outcome (:226-228), and a restart leaves completed entries alone (:341-355).
  So the instrument always has evidence of the completion. That leaves three
  crash windows:

  Window: W1: plate read completed, crash before the S3 upload
  What survives: Ledger: completed + SHA-256. The data bytes are gone, because
  they only existed in driver memory
  True record: That attempt failed ("data lost before storage"). Retry the read.

  The ledger will honestly show two executions
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Window: W2: uploaded, crash before JetStream acknowledges
  What survives: Object at the fixed key, hash matches the ledger
  True record: Recoverable: completed
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Window: W3: a step with no data completed, crash before the event
  What survives: Ledger: completed
  True record: Recoverable: completed

  Recovery uses two paths, on purpose:
  - R1. Replay when the driver starts. It reads the ledger and republishes
    finished for recent executions, checking storage for plate data. Repeats are
    harmless because of message-ID dedupe and idempotent apply.
  - R2. Executor deadline plus reconcile query. This covers a driver that stays
    down or restarts without anyone noticing.

  Both depend on the instrument's own durable record.

  Remaining limitations

  Accepted, and written up in the note:
  - W1 always costs a re-read. No software can close it, because the bytes
    existed only in RAM.
  - After a driver crash, the outcome is only as durable as the instrument's 
    history. A real device without a ledger leaves us with "outcome unknown",
    and for the liquid handler that needs a human.
  - Command expiry relies on clocks agreeing. The containers share the host
    clock. Production would need NTP, or a "claim before running" check with the
    executor.
  - The dedupe window is 2 minutes. Longer outages rely on idempotent apply.
  - The driver holds an event in memory until JetStream acknowledges it. A crash
    in that window is recovered by R1 or R2.
  - The measurements come from Docker Desktop on a Mac. Real networks may behave
    differently.
  - A simulator artifact: a driver crash during a job kills the simulated work,
    and the ledger marks it hung. A real instrument would carry on.

  Out of scope:
  - A single NATS server with a single-copy stream. If NATS is down, nothing
    moves. That fails safe, but it isn't highly available.
  - A single executor. Replicas would need per-run locking.

  The evidence is in tmp/_natsprobe/: the probe, run.sh, and the logs in out/.

  Next steps

  1. Record D1: C, amended with command expiry, "a timeout means unknown",
     retries with the same ID, idempotent apply, explicit pings, and recovery
     paths R1 and R2. I'd write it in docs/decisions.md on a small branch and
     open a docs-only PR. OK to go ahead?
  2. V2 page: a subagent is building tmp/d1-messaging-v2.html from V1. V1 stays
     unchanged. I'll check it against these findings and open it when it's done.

✻ Baked for 8m 30s · done Monday 01:42

※ recap: We're designing the executor-to-driver messaging for the lab automation challenge, and 
  you chose option C, amended by the disconnect experiment and shown in the V2 page. Next step: 
  your OK to record that as decision D1 in a docs-only PR.

❯ clarify a few cases before we record: how to prevent commands from running    
  after cancellation even if they haven't expired, what happens when the driver 
  stays offline and reconciliation isn't possible, and how to distinguish a     
  completed physical read from missing data.                                    

⏺ Each of these needs a clear rule before we record D1. Here's my answer to
  each, with the amendment it makes to C. All of this is inferred from the
  design and the code. Nothing here has been measured yet.

  1. Stopping a command from running after a cancel, even if it hasn't expired

  The gap: the experiment showed commands arriving up to 20 s late (F1). With
  expires_at = now + 5s, a cancel at 1 s and a command delivered at 3 s, the
  driver would still start the job. Expiry makes the window smaller but doesn't
  close it.

  The fix: the driver claims the job before running it. Before inst.Run, the
  driver sends a request to lab.executor.claim {job_id}. The executor does one
  conditional update in Postgres:

  UPDATE steps SET phase = 'claimed'
   WHERE job_id = $1 AND phase = 'dispatched'
     AND run is not cancelled         -- same row/transaction as CancelRun's 
  update

  - One row changed: reply go, and the driver starts the job.
  - No row changed: reply abort (cancelled, superseded, or unknown), and the
    driver does nothing.
  - Timeout or no reply: don't run. The driver retries the claim with the same
    job_id. The claim is idempotent: asking again for an already-claimed job
    gets go again.

  Why this guarantees it: cancel and claim both change the same step row, so
  Postgres puts them in order and exactly one of them wins.
  - The claim wins: the job starts. The cancel can no longer stop it, so the
    record shows its real outcome. A cancel stops what hasn't started, and
    that's the only promise we can keep with physical equipment.
  - The cancel wins: the claim is refused, and nothing physical happens.

  What could go wrong:
  - The go reply is lost. The database says claimed but the driver didn't run
    the job. The deadline expires, and the reconcile query answers "never ran"
    (there's no ledger entry for this job_id). The executor then returns the
    step to dispatched and resends the same job_id. The ledger proves nothing
    ran, so this is safe.
  - A claim arrives late, after the driver gave up. This is the same case, and
    it's handled the same way.
  - The executor is down. Nothing new starts. That's fail-safe, and the executor
    is the only one who issues commands anyway.

  Side effect: the claim removes the need for expires_at, and with it the
  clock-agreement limitation. I'd drop expiry, or keep it only as a hint so
  drivers don't bother asking about very old commands.

  The cost is one extra round trip, a few milliseconds, before each physical
  start. I think a lab system should pay that.

  2. The driver stays offline and reconciliation isn't possible

  Whether a claim was granted decides what we know.

  ┌─────────────┬─────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │    When     │                         │                                 │
  │ contact was │      What we know       │           Record says           │
  │     lost    │                         │                                 │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Before the  │ Nothing physical ran.   │ Step pending. The run waits,    │
  │ claim was   │ Our own database proves │ and other branches continue. A  │
  │ granted     │  it.                    │ cancel turns it cancelled,      │
  │             │                         │ truthfully.                     │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ After the   │ It may be running, or   │ Step running, with error =      │
  │ claim was   │ may have finished,      │ "driver unreachable since       │
  │ granted     │ failed or hung. We      │ 10:42, outcome unknown". The    │
  │             │ can't tell.             │ run stays running.              │
  └─────────────┴─────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  The rule: unknown stays unknown. After a deadline, it's tempting to mark the
  step failed, but if the job actually completed, the record would then
  contradict the ledger. A scientist would also read "failed" as "the plate
  wasn't filled", which could be false. So we surface the unknown rather than
  resolve it with a guess:
  - Internally, the step's phase is unknown, along with the time we last heard
    from the driver.
  - The REST API shows it as running, with the note in error.
  - GraphQL can expose it properly later, for example outcomeKnown: false and
    lastContactAt.

  Late facts always win. When the driver comes back, replay (R1) or the
  reconcile query (R2) brings in the real outcome:
  - Hours later, after the 2-minute dedupe window, the guarded update still
    applies it: a step can move from in-flight to its outcome, never backwards.
  - If the run was cancelled in the meantime, the physical fact is still 
    recorded. A cancel never overwrites a step that was running.

  What could go wrong:
  - A run can stay running forever if the driver never returns. That's honest,
    but it doesn't resolve anything. In production, the way out is an operator
    action, something like "declare the step lost, with a reason". The record
    would show it as a statement by a person, not a fact from the instrument.
    I'd list that as out of scope, or as a GraphQL mutation if time allows.
  - A cancelled run with an unknown step: steps that were never claimed become
    cancelled. The run itself only reaches cancelled once nothing is unknown.

  3. Telling a completed physical read apart from missing data

  Two independent sources of evidence decide the outcome: the ledger (via the
  driver) says the read physically happened and gives its SHA-256, and storage
  has, or doesn't have, the object at the fixed key
  runs/<run>/<step>/<job_id>.csv.

  Ledger: completed, sha X
  Object at key: exists, hash X
  Driver: any
  Step: completed, data_ref set
  Why: Both sources agree
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ledger: completed, sha X
  Object at key: missing
  Driver: alive, still has the bytes
  Step: running (phase storing), note "storage unavailable, retrying"
  Why: The read happened, the data isn't safe yet
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ledger: completed, sha X
  Object at key: missing
  Driver: restarted, bytes gone (W1)
  Step: attempt failed: "data lost before storage". Retry the read.
  Why: We know the data is lost
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ledger: completed, sha X
  Object at key: exists, hash ≠ X
  Driver: any
  Step: not completed. Re-upload if the driver still has the bytes, otherwise
  failed "stored data does not match the instrument"
  Why: Should never happen, but we'd detect it
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ledger: failed / hung
  Object at key: —
  Driver: any
  Step: failed or retried, per the hang/failure policy
  Why: No data claim
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ledger: —
  Object at key: —
  Driver: unreachable
  Step: running / unknown (case 2)
  Why: Can't tell "storing" from "lost"

  The executor checks independently. The driver's finished event carries the
  ledger's data_sha256, which the driver reads from inst.Status(). The executor
  then reads the object itself and hashes it before setting data_ref and
  completed. That way, completed is backed by two independent sources rather
  than by the driver's word. The cost is one small (~1.6 KB) download per read.
  If storage is down, the step stays in storing, which is the honest answer.

  To show progress, the driver can publish a measured event when the physical
  read finishes, before the upload. The record can then say "read done, storing
  data", rather than leaving running with no explanation.

  What could go wrong:
  - A re-read after data loss creates a second ledger execution for that step.
    That's honest, but it fails acceptance check 5 ("no step ran more than
    once"). Under chaos that's expected, and the note will say so.
  - Re-reading a plate measures it again, so the values may differ in reality.
    The simulator derives the values from the job ID, so a new attempt gives new
    data. The record has to show which attempt the data came from.

  What this adds to D1

  1. Claim before run. It's the single point where cancel and start are put in
     order, and a timeout means don't run. expires_at is no longer needed for
     correctness.
  2. Unknown stays unknown. After a claim, a lost driver means running with an
     "outcome unknown" note, never a guessed outcome. Late facts always win,
     even after a cancel.
  3. A read is completed only when both sources agree. The ledger SHA must equal
     the hash of the stored object, checked by the executor. Lost data is a
     failed attempt and gets retried.
  4. Internal phases: dispatched → claimed → running → storing → completed | 
     failed | unknown. They map to the fixed API statuses: dispatched shows as
     pending, everything from claimed to storing plus unknown shows as running,
     and the scheduler treats a dispatched step as occupying its device.

  Each of these becomes a check in a later slice. For example, a database test
  for the claim/cancel race, a reconcile test for "claimed but never ran", and a
  /break that disconnects the plate reader between the read and the upload.

  Your call:
  1. Claim before run: do you agree with this approach? The alternative is
     broadcasting cancels, which is faster but can't guarantee anything, because
     it races the same way.
  2. A permanently offline driver: leave it as an honest "unknown", or include
     an operator "declare lost" action?
  3. Record D1: if both are settled, I'll write D1 with these amendments in
     docs/decisions.md and open the docs-only PR.

⏺ Breaking D1's happy path into slices

  Each slice is one PR with one responsibility. Each depends only on slices
  before it, and each can be tested without the ones after it.

  #: S1
  Slice: Step lifecycle (pure)
  Responsibility: Which facts can change a step, and how. Maps internal phases
  to
  the fixed API statuses
  Depends on: —
  Check: go test: table-driven, no I/O
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: S2
  Slice: Schema + step store
  Responsibility: db/init.sql phases and columns. Apply a fact inside a
  row-locked transaction. GetRun maps phases to statuses
  Depends on: S1
  Check: go test against the compose Postgres. API shape unchanged
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: S3
  Slice: Message contract
  Responsibility: Shared package: subjects, payload types, JobID format,
  message-ID helper
  Depends on: —
  Check: go test: JobID round-trip, JSON shape
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: S4
  Slice: Driver: command → claim → run → events
  Responsibility: Serve: ledger check, accepted/busy/known, claim, inst.Run,
  publish to JetStream with retries. No plate data yet
  Depends on: S3
  Check: Driver test against real NATS with a fake executor. The ledger shows
  exactly one run per job ID
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: S5
  Slice: Executor: dispatch + claim + consume
  Responsibility: Provision the stream, dispatch loop with NextSteps, claim
  handler, event consumer, run status
  Depends on: S1, S2, S3
  Check: acceptance.sh: checks 1–6 pass, check 7 (plate data) still fails
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: S6
  Slice: Plate data end to end
  Responsibility: Driver uploads to the fixed key before finished. Executor
  checks the hash, then sets data_ref
  Depends on: S4, S5
  Check: acceptance.sh: 7/7

  Hardening, after the happy path:
  - the truth-checker script;
  - R2 (deadlines and the reconcile query), together with the unknown phase;
  - R1 (driver replay on startup);
  - cancel;
  - hang and retry policy, which needs a /decide first.

  S4 is where the in-process NATS server may finally be needed. We'll decide
  then, with a concrete test in front of us.

  Framing S1: the step lifecycle

  Behaviour: given the facts we've received about a step, its record shows only
  what the evidence supports. Duplicate, stale or out-of-order facts change
  nothing. Physical facts are never overwritten by intent. A read is only
  completed once its data is verified.

  Why first: every later slice calls this function, and every truth rule in D1
  lives in it. It's pure (no NATS, no Postgres), so it's the cheapest place to
  get the rules right and the easiest to review. Nothing calls it yet, so it
  can't break anything.

  The data shape (new files services/executor/lifecycle.go and
  lifecycle_test.go):

  type Phase string // pending, dispatched, claimed, running, storing, 
  completed, failed, cancelled

  type Step struct {
      Phase        Phase
      JobID        string // current attempt's job; facts for any other job are 
  stale
      Attempt      int
      ProducesData bool
      StartedAt, FinishedAt *time.Time // from the instrument's ledger, not our 
  clock
      Error        *string
      LedgerSHA    *string // what the instrument says it measured
      DataRef      *string
  }

  // Facts: Dispatched{JobID, Attempt} · Claimed{JobID} · Started{JobID, At}
  //        Finished{JobID, Outcome, At, Error, SHA} · DataStored{JobID, Ref, 
  SHA} · Cancel{}
  func Apply(s Step, f Fact) (Step, bool)  // new step, and whether anything 
  changed
  func (p Phase) APIStatus() string        // dispatched→pending; 
  claimed/running/storing→running

  The test is table-driven. Each row gives a starting step, a fact, the expected
  step, and whether it changed:

  1. Happy path, step with no data: pending → dispatched → claimed → running →
     completed. The times come from the facts.
  2. Read with data: Finished{completed, SHA X} moves to storing, not completed.
     Then DataStored{SHA X} moves to completed with data_ref. DataStored{SHA Y}
     doesn't complete it.
  3. Duplicate: applying the same fact twice gives changed=false the second
     time.
  4. Stale attempt: a fact for a different job_id is ignored.
  5. No going backwards: for example, Started after Finished, or Claimed after
     running, is ignored.
  6. Cancel vs physical work: Cancel moves pending or dispatched to cancelled.
     On claimed, running or storing it changes nothing.
  7. Claim after cancel is refused. This is D1's claim/cancel ordering, at the
     model level. The database makes it safe under concurrency in S2.
  8. Late facts win: a step that is running when its run is cancelled still
     records its real Finished.
  9. API mapping: each phase maps to exactly one of the five fixed statuses. A
     dispatched step occupies its device for NextSteps.

  Deliberately left out of S1:
  - unknown and "claimed but never ran" (go to S7, with the reconcile query);
  - retries and attempt numbering policy (go to the hang/retry /decide);
  - run-level status (goes to S5).

  A failed or hung Finished simply means failed for now.

  Size: about 120 lines of code plus the tests.

  What could go wrong with this slice itself:
  - Rules on paper don't guarantee rules in production. A pure function can't
    stop two concurrent writers. That's S2's row lock, and the test only proves
    rule 7 when facts are applied one after another.
  - A rule we get wrong here gets built into every later slice. That's why I'd
    like you to review the table above, row by row, before any code exists.

  Depends on: D1 (the truth rules and internal phases). No other decision is
  needed.

  OK to go ahead with S1 as framed? Or would you change any row: for example,
  should a mismatched hash make the step failed straight away instead of leaving
  it in storing?

❯ S1 looks good, a head.                                                        
  To clarify, A hung job doesn't necessarily mean failed, and we should handle  
  or clearly account for facts arriving out of order, especially data being     
  stored before the finished event.                                             
  For a hash mismatch, keep the step unresolved until there's enough evidence   
  to determine the outcome.                                                     

⏺ Got it: a hung job and a hash mismatch both stay unresolved until there's
  evidence, and data stored before finished must work. That changes the core
  design, so here's the adjustment before I code.

  Adjustment: separate the two kinds of fact.
  - Intent (dispatched, claimed, cancel) is ours. Postgres orders it, and order
    matters: a claim and a cancel decide each other.
  - Evidence (started, finished, data stored) comes from the instrument and from
    storage. It can arrive in any order, so it's merged regardless of order,
    and the phase is derived from what we know. It isn't a chain of transitions
    that out-of-order facts could trip over.

  So "data stored before finished" means the step stays running until finished
  brings the ledger's SHA, then it's compared. hung, a hash mismatch, or two
  conflicting outcomes for the same job each give a new phase, unresolved, with
  a reason. The API shows it as running, with the reason in error.

⏺ S1 is built, its tests pass, and Codex has reviewed it. It's committed on
  slice/step-lifecycle but not pushed. There's no PR yet, because two questions
  below need your answer first.

  How it works

  The code is in services/executor/lifecycle.go. Nothing calls it yet, so it
  can't affect the running system.

  - A Step holds two kinds of state (:41):
    - Intent: JobID, Attempt, Claimed, Cancelled. This is ours.
    - Evidence: ledger times, Outcome, LedgerSHA, and the last storage check
      (StoredSHA, StoredRef, StoredAt), plus a Conflict note.
  - Apply(step, fact) returns the new step and whether anything changed (:97).
  - Intent facts are applied in order:
    - Dispatched only sets the job ID once (:100).
    - Claimed is refused after a cancel (:104).
    - Cancel is refused once the step is claimed or has any evidence (:108).
  - Evidence facts are merged so the order doesn't matter:
    - Started fills the start time once (:114).
    - The first Finished wins. A different outcome for the same job is recorded
      as a conflict, not an overwrite (:118).
    - For DataStored, the newest storage check wins, whatever order the checks
      arrive in (:128).
    - Evidence for a different job ID is ignored.
  - Phase() is derived, not stored (:142). Evidence comes first, intent second.
    Reason() (:169) explains an unresolved phase: hung, a hash mismatch, no
    digest from the instrument, or conflicting outcomes.
  - The API view:
    - APIStatus (:183) maps the nine phases onto the five fixed statuses.
    - SchedulerStatus (:195) treats a dispatched step as running, so NextSteps
      won't give its device to another step.
    - APIError (:202) carries the reason, or the instrument's error.
    - APIDataRef (:215) only appears once the data has been checked.

  Why this way

  Your two clarifications showed that a plain state machine was the wrong shape.
  If the phase were updated one transition at a time, a fact arriving out of
  order (data before finished, started after finished) would need special
  handling for each combination. Deriving the phase from evidence collected in
  any order makes ordering bugs impossible by construction, and
  TestEvidenceOrderDoesNotMatter proves it for all 24 orderings.

  The one place where order is supposed to matter is claim versus cancel. That's
  intent, and it stays ordered.

  Evidence (measured)

  - Against the stub functions, the table test failed on all 23 cases that
    needed real behaviour. The 24th, "new step is pending", passes by default.
    That shows the tests check behaviour, not just that the code compiles.
  - After the implementation:
    - go test -count=1 ./services/executor/ → ok, 6 tests, including 24 table
      cases and 24 orderings.
    - go build ./..., go vet ./... and gofmt -l are clean.
  - Size: 221 lines of code, of which roughly 90 are types and constants, and
    205 lines of tests. That's above the 150-line guideline, but it's one idea
    and has no dependencies.

  Codex finding (1): it needs your call

  [P2] Data that can't be recovered never becomes failed. This is the W1 case:
  the read completed, then the driver crashed before uploading. The ledger says
  completed, the data is gone for good, and the step sits in storing forever,
  shown as running with no error. Codex is right. D1 says this case is a failed
  attempt ("data lost before storage"), and S1 has no fact that can say so.

  Proposed fix (about 10 lines plus 2 test cases): a DataLost{JobID} evidence
  fact, sent by the driver when it no longer has the bytes.
  - The completed physical outcome is kept, and the phase becomes failed with
    "data lost before storage".
  - If a matching DataStored also arrives, in any order, it wins and the step is
    completed. That covers W2, where the upload actually succeeded.

  This stays order-independent. Fix it in this slice, or defer it to the replay 
  slice (R1)? I'd fix it now, because it's a truth rule and it's small.

  My own review: one finding that needs your call

  A newer storage check can move a completed step back to unresolved. In the
  API, that's completed → running. That's truthful if storage later holds data
  that doesn't match. But observers would see a status go backwards, and
  dependent steps may already have started. Options:
  - (a) Keep it as it is. The record is true, but a status can regress.
  - (b) Once completed, ignore later storage checks for the step. Corruption
    that happens after we checked would then go unnoticed.

  I lean towards (a). In practice, only S6's own check produces this fact, so it
  would only regress if storage changed underneath us.

  Still open, and deliberately deferred (each is noted for its slice):
  - Concurrent writers: S2's row lock.
  - Retries: a step currently holds a single attempt.
  - unknown after contact is lost: S7.
  - A Finished for an earlier attempt is dropped, not kept as history.

  Questions to check your understanding

  1. Why is it safe for DataStored to arrive before Finished, but not for
     Claimed to arrive after Cancel?
  2. Which test would fail if Apply let a second Finished overwrite the first,
     and what would the record wrongly claim?

  Next: tell me (1) fix DataLost now or defer, and (2) (a) or (b). Then I'll
  apply them, re-run the checks and open the PR.

  One more thing: tmp/ now also has exp-instrument/, exp-nats-handler/ and
  smoke/, which I didn't create. go vet ./... picks up tmp/smoke, because ./...
  only skips folders whose names start with _. I haven't touched them.

⏺ Two changes. First, a DataLost fact. Second, a rule for the storage check: a 
  verified completion is never undone, and a later mismatch is reported but 
  doesn't reverse it.

  The reasoning behind the second: "the step completed and its data was verified
  at time T" stays true forever. Corruption found later is a new fact about the
  stored copy, not about the step. So the status stays completed, error carries
  "stored data no longer matches what the instrument measured (checked at T)",
  and data_ref stays, so the scientist can still find the object.

  This also needs the storage evidence to be a set of checks, not just "the
  latest". Whether any check ever matched is then independent of arrival order,
  even if the ledger's SHA arrives last.
