⏺ Both breakages were caught by the new tests, and the restored code passes. The
  storage-check rule is a new decision, so it gets a short entry in the
  decisions log:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Both changes are in and tested. S1 is two commits on slice/step-lifecycle, not
  pushed yet.

  What changed

  1. DataLost (Codex's finding, now fixed).
  - The fact: a new evidence fact, DataLost{JobID}, meaning "the driver no
    longer has the bytes".
  - The rule:
    - With no matching copy in storage, the read is failed, and the error says
      "instrument completed the read, but its data was lost before storage". If
      a mismatching copy exists, the error adds "the stored object does not
      match".
    - The instrument's completed outcome is kept in the step. The record doesn't
      pretend the read never happened.
  - The W2 case: if the upload actually succeeded and a matching copy shows up,
    in any order, the step is completed. Being lost is only final when storage
    can't prove otherwise.

  2. Storage checks: a third option between (a) and (b), recorded as D2.
  - The rule: "completed, data verified at T" stays true forever. So once any
    read-back of storage has matched the ledger, the step stays completed and
    keeps its data_ref.
  - A later mismatching check shows up in error as "stored data no longer
    matches what the instrument measured (checked T)". A newer matching check
    clears it.
  - Result: corruption isn't hidden, no status moves backwards, and dependent
    steps aren't contradicted.
  - The data change behind it: storage evidence is now a set of checks, not just
    the latest one. Whether a check ever matched then doesn't depend on arrival
    order, even if the ledger's SHA arrives last.
  - Before verification, a mismatch is still unresolved. With DataLost as well,
    it's failed.

  Evidence (measured)

  - go build, go vet and go test -count=1 ./services/... are clean. 8 tests
    pass, including the order-independence check run over every ordering for
    four scenarios.
  - I broke each rule on purpose to check the tests can fail. I put the code
    back afterwards:
    - Ignoring DataLost failed 3 table cases, TestDataLostIsExplained and the
      order test.
    - Counting only the latest check (the old behaviour) failed 2 table cases,
      TestVerifiedCompletionStaysCompleted and the order test.
  - Size: lifecycle.go is now 276 lines, well over the ~150 guideline. A lot of
    it is types, constants and comments, and it's still one idea with no
    dependencies, so I kept it as one PR.
  - Codex: I haven't run a second review. The fix came from its one review. Say
    if you want a second pass on the final diff.

  What could still go wrong

  - error now carries three different things: an unresolved reason, a failure
    reason, or a warning on a completed step. Tools reading only status won't
    see a corruption warning. GraphQL can give data integrity its own field
    later (noted in D2).
  - Who sends DataLost: the driver, during replay (R1), when it finds a
    completed read in the ledger with no copy in storage and no bytes in memory.
    If it sends DataLost wrongly while the upload is merely slow, a later
    matching DataStored still wins. So a false DataLost costs time, not truth.
  - Still deferred: the row lock (S2), retries (a step holds a single attempt),
    and unknown after contact is lost (S7).

  Questions to check your understanding

  1. A check finds a mismatch at 10:00, and a check at 09:59 found a match, but
     they arrive in that order. What does the record say, and why does the
     arrival order not matter?
  2. Why does DataLost make a mismatched read failed, when without it the same
     evidence stays unresolved?

  OK to push and open the S1 PR into dev?

      30 +func (e *Executor) apply(ctx context.Context, where string, arg
         +s []any, f Fact) (Step, bool, error) {                         
      31 +  tx, err := e.db.BeginTx(ctx, nil)                            
      32 +  if err != nil {

      34 +  }                                                            
      35 +  defer tx.Rollback()

         +FOR UPDATE`, args...).Scan(&runID, &name, &raw)

         +rgs)

      59 +  if err := tx.Commit(); err != nil {

  Created PR #5, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
⏺ S2 is open for review as PR #5:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/5

  Every fact about a step is now saved in Postgres. Two writers can't overwrite
  each other, and the API shape hasn't changed. Before your next stack run,
  you'll need to recreate the stack's database (details below).

  How it works

  - The schema (db/init.sql): steps gains lifecycle JSONB, which holds S1's Step
    and is the source of truth. It also gains phase and job_id UNIQUE, used for
    lookups. The existing status, started_at, finished_at, error and data_ref
    columns are now always derived from lifecycle.
  - Two entry points (store.go:17, :23):
    - applyToStep(run, name, fact) handles our own intent (dispatch, cancel).
    - applyToJob(job_id, fact) handles whatever a driver tells us (claims,
      evidence).
  - Both go through apply (store.go:30), which runs one transaction:
    a. SELECT … FOR UPDATE loads the row and locks it (:40).
    b. S1's Apply merges the fact in.
    c. If nothing changed, it returns without writing (:53).
    d. Otherwise saveStep (:65) writes the lifecycle and every derived column in
       one UPDATE, and the transaction commits.
  - CreateRun (service.go:68) stores each step's initial lifecycle, including
    whether it produces data.
  - GetRun is untouched. It reads the same columns as before, and they're now
    always consistent with the lifecycle.

  Why this way

  - The lock is the guarantee. A second writer waits on the row lock, then reads
    the first writer's committed result. Facts are never lost, and a claim and
    a cancel are put in order: exactly one wins (D1).
  - The derived columns are written in the same transaction, so the API can
    never show a status the lifecycle doesn't support. That's also why GetRun
    didn't need to change.
  - The whole Step is stored as JSON rather than one column per field. The rules
    live in one pure function, and the store only needs load–apply–save.
    Optimistic version checks with retries would give the same guarantee with
    more code.

  Evidence (measured)

  - go build, go vet and go test -count=1 ./services/... are clean: 15 tests, 7
    of them new integration tests. Each test runs in a throwaway database built
    from the real db/init.sql.
  - Against the stub, all 7 new tests failed with "not implemented".
  - Without FOR UPDATE, the race test failed 5 times out of 5. Each time, on the
    second of its 40 races, both the claim and the cancel "won". With the lock,
    it passed 3 times out of 3.
  - psql applied init.sql to a scratch database, and the new columns and unique
    constraint appeared. I dropped that database afterwards.
  - TestAPIShapeUnchanged checks the exact JSON keys of a run and a step through
    the real HTTP handlers.
  - Codex: no findings on a static read. Its sandbox couldn't run the tests.

  Before your next stack run

  The live executor_db still has the old schema: I checked, and the lifecycle
  column is missing. You'll need to run docker compose down -v once. That wipes
  local runs, ledgers and the NATS stream data, which is why I didn't run it.

  Non-blocking items, logged in docs/concerns.md

  - K9: whether a step produces data is decided by device name.
  - K10: the lifecycle JSON has no version, so renaming a field would silently
    drop it from stored rows.
  - K11: S5's event consumer must ack only after applyToJob returns
    successfully.
  - K12: any future transaction that locks several steps must lock them in a
    fixed order, to avoid deadlocks.

  Questions to check your understanding

  1. Without FOR UPDATE, how can both the claim and the cancel succeed, and what
     would the driver and the record each believe afterwards?
  2. Why is it safe for apply to return without writing when nothing changed,
     even though the caller may be retrying a fact it isn't sure was saved?

  Your .gitignore change is still uncommitted and isn't in the PR.

  When you merge #5, the next slice is S3, the message contract: subjects,
  payload types, the JobID format and the message-ID helper. It's small and has
  no dependencies, so it could be built alongside S2's review.

      110 +  StartedAt  *time.Time `json:"started_at,omitempty"`  // ledg
          +er time                                                       
      111 +  FinishedAt *time.Time `json:"finished_at,omitempty"` // ledg
          +er time                                                       
      112 +  Outcome    string     `json:"outcome,omitempty"`     // comp
          +leted | failed | hung, as in the ledger                       
      113 +  Error      string     `json:"error,omitempty"`              
      114 +  SHA        string     `json:"sha256,omitempty"` // ledger da
          +ta_sha256                                                     
      115 +  Ref        string     `json:"ref,omitempty"`    // s3://buck
          +et/key                                                        
      116 +}                                                             
      117 +                                                              
      118 +func (e Event) Validate() error {                             
      119 +  if e.V != Version {                                         
      120 +    return fmt.Errorf("event version %d, want %d", e.V, Versio
          +n)                                                            
      121 +  }                                                           
      122 +  if e.Device == "" || e.JobID == "" {                        
      123 +    return errors.New("event needs device and job_id")        
      124 +  }                                                           
      125 +  switch e.Kind {                                             
      126 +  case KindStarted:                                           
      127 +    if e.StartedAt == nil {                                   
      128 +      return errors.New("started event needs started_at")     
      129 +    }                                                         
      130 +  case KindFinished:                                          
      131 +    if e.StartedAt == nil || e.FinishedAt == nil {            
      132 +      return errors.New("finished event needs started_at and f
          +inished_at")                                                  
      133 +    }                                                         
      134 +    switch e.Outcome {                                        
      135 +    case "completed", "failed", "hung":                       
      136 +    default:                                                  
      137 +      return fmt.Errorf("finished event has outcome %q", e.Out
          +come)                                                         
      138 +    }                                                         
      139 +  case KindStored:                                            
      140 +    if !strings.HasPrefix(e.Ref, "s3://") {                   
      141 +      return fmt.Errorf("stored event needs an s3:// ref, got 
          +%q", e.Ref)                                                   
      142 +    }                                                         
      143 +  case KindDataLost:                                          
      144 +  default:                                                    
      145 +    return fmt.Errorf("unknown event kind %q", e.Kind)        
      146 +  }                                                           
      147 +  return nil                                                  
      148 +}                                                             
      149 +                                                              
      150 +func (m RunCommand) version() int   { return m.V }            
      151 +func (m RunReply) version() int     { return m.V }            
      152 +func (m ClaimRequest) version() int { return m.V }            
      153 +func (m ClaimReply) version() int   { return m.V }            
      154 +func (m Event) version() int        { return m.V }            
      155 +                                                              
      156 +type message interface{ version() int }                       
      157 +                                                              
      158 +// Decode parses a message and rejects any version but this on
          +e. Events still                                               
      159 +// need Validate.                                             
      160 +func Decode[M message](data []byte, m *M) error {             
      161 +  if err := json.Unmarshal(data, m); err != nil {             
      162 +    return fmt.Errorf("decode %T: %w", *m, err)               
      163 +  }                                                           
      164 +  if v := (*m).version(); v != Version {                      
      165 +    return fmt.Errorf("decode %T: version %d, want %d", *m, v,
          + Version)                                                     
      166 +  }                                                           
      167 +  return nil                                                  
      168 +}

⏺ S3 is open for review as PR #6:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/6. I haven't reset
  the database.

  PR #5 (S2) isn't merged yet. GitHub still shows it as open, and dev is at the
  PR #4 merge. S3 is a separate package that doesn't depend on S2, so I built it
  on the current dev. The two PRs can be merged in either order.

  How it works

  services/common/protocol/protocol.go is the only place either side gets
  subjects, IDs or message types from. The header comment (:1-7) shows the three
  exchanges:

  1. The executor sends a run command to the driver as a request on
     lab.<device>.cmd.run (RunSubject, :41). The driver replies accepted, busy
     or known.
  2. Before any physical work, the driver asks to claim the job on
     lab.executor.claim. The executor replies go or abort.
  3. The driver publishes events to lab.<device>.evt.<kind> (:42). The stream
     LAB_EVENTS captures them with the filter lab.*.evt.* (:37). Each event's
     Nats-Msg-Id is MsgID(job, kind) (:53), so JetStream only drops a true
     repeat of the same event.

  Three helpers produce the IDs and keys:
  - JobID (:47): run/step/attempt. The ledger records it, which is how the
    driver recognises a repeated command.
  - DataKey (:58): runs/<job_id>.csv. It's derived from the job ID, so the
    executor can check storage without being told where to look, and a retried
    upload overwrites the same object.
  - NewRunCommand (:68): builds a run command with the version and job ID filled
    in.

  Every message is versioned. Decode (:162) rejects any version but 1, and
  Event.Validate (:120) checks that each kind has the fields it needs. For
  example, a finished event needs both ledger times and an outcome of completed,
  failed or hung. A stored event needs an s3:// ref.

  Why this way

  - The four event kinds map one to one onto S1's evidence facts, so S5's event
    consumer is a simple translation. finished is sent as soon as the instrument
    returns, and stored after the upload. Between the two, the step is honestly
    storing. That's why D1's separate "measured" event is no longer needed.
  - Validation happens at the boundary. Malformed or unknown-version messages
    are rejected when they're decoded, so the step lifecycle can trust its
    inputs.

  Evidence (measured)

  - go vet and go test -count=1 ./services/... are clean. All 5 tests failed
    against the stub functions and pass now:
    - Subjects: exact strings. Every event subject matches the stream filter,
      and commands and claims don't, so they never end up stored in the stream.
    - IDs: distinct by run, step, attempt and kind. The same event always gets
      the same message ID.
    - Wire shape: the exact JSON of all five message types, so a renamed field
      fails a test rather than failing in production.
    - Versions: v2, a missing v, and malformed JSON are all rejected.
    - Validation: 4 valid and 8 invalid events, each correctly accepted or
      rejected.
  - Codex: no findings. Its sandbox couldn't run the tests.

  Concerns (non-blocking)

  These are in the PR description. They'll go into docs/concerns.md once #5 is
  merged, to avoid a merge conflict, since both PRs append to the same file.
  - K13: device names are used in subjects without being checked.
  - K14: there's no plan for rolling upgrades between message versions.
  - K15: S5 should check that an event's device matches the device in its
    subject.

  Questions to check your understanding

  1. Why does MsgID include the event kind, and what would go wrong if it were
     only the job ID?
  2. A stored event arrives saying the data is at
     s3://lab-data/runs/run-1/read_plate/1.csv. Why doesn't the executor just
     believe it and mark the step completed?

  Next: S4, the driver side, which uses this package. It's also the first slice
  that needs real NATS in its tests, so we'll decide then whether to use the
  compose server or the in-process one.

       63 +  var cmd protocol.RunCommand

          +)

          + RunID: cmd.RunID, StepName: cmd.Step})

          +ment.Execution) bool { return true })

          + {

          +sion, JobID: jobID, Device: d.device})

          +bject, req)

          +ice, jobID, reply.Reason)

          + ran <-chan error, cond func(instrument.Execution) bool) (inst
          +rument.Execution, bool) {

  Created PR #7, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ S4 is open for review as PR #7:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/7

  The driver now runs each job at most once, only after the executor's go, and
  reports how it ended from the instrument's ledger. Plate data isn't stored
  yet: that's S6.

  How it works

  The code is in services/driver/adapter.go.

  1. A run command arrives on lab.<device>.cmd.run (Serve :36, onRun :62).
     accept (:80) answers immediately, under a lock:
     - known: this job is already in flight, or the ledger already has it (:84).
       It is never run twice.
     - busy: anything else is running. Nothing runs.
     - accepted: the job becomes the single job in flight (:90).
  2. The driver claims the job (execute :94, claim :125) before touching the
     instrument.
     - go: the job runs.
     - abort: nothing runs, and the device is free again.
     - No answer: it asks again every 500 ms, and never takes silence as a yes.
  3. inst.Run runs in the background (:101). The driver watches the ledger, not
     Run's return (waitLedger :150):
     - when the job's ledger entry appears, it publishes started, with the
       ledger's start time;
     - when the entry's outcome is no longer running (:111), it publishes
       finished, with the ledger's times, outcome, error and SHA.

     A hung job never returns from Run, but its ledger entry becomes hung after
     HANG_DURATION, so it's still reported.
  4. Publishing (publish :171) retries until JetStream acknowledges. Every retry
     carries the same Nats-Msg-Id, because C1 showed that a timed-out publish
     may already have been stored.

  Why this way

  - The ledger is the evidence, and Run's return value is only a convenience.
    That one choice covers hangs, and it keeps the reported times in the
    instrument's clock.
  - Only one job can be in flight. The instrument does one thing at a time
    anyway, so the driver says busy up front instead of finding out from ErrBusy
    after the claim.
  - The tests use an in-process nats-server. This is the dependency you'd held
    back until there was a concrete need, and this was it. On the compose NATS,
    the tests' LAB_EVENTS stream would clash with the real stack's, since
    JetStream forbids two streams with overlapping subjects. It's test-only and
    pinned to the compose server's version.

  Evidence (measured)

  - All 8 tests failed against the stub Serve, and all pass now. Run 5 times
    under -race: 40 runs, all passed, no data races.
  - Breaking the rules on purpose:
    - Treating an unanswered claim as go: the job ran with no permission (ran 1 
      times without a claim answer).
    - Skipping the ledger check: a repeated command was accepted and ran a 
      second time. That's exactly what acceptance check 5 catches.
  - go vet and go test ./services/... are clean across all three packages.

  Codex: 3 findings, none blocking this slice

  Finding: The plate reader's data is dropped, so reads stay storing
  Triage: Deferred to S6 (planned). Logged as K17
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Finding: A driver restart between the ledger outcome and the acknowledged
  finished loses the event; a repeated command then gets known and nothing
  reports it
  Triage: Deferred to R1 (planned): replay the ledger on startup and on known.
  Logged as K18
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Finding: If the instrument's final ledger write fails, our completed and the
  ledger file disagree after a restart
  Triage: Accepted. It needs a disk error, and we can't see it from outside the

  Created PR #8, ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ S5a, the executor's receive side, is done and open as PR #8 into dev. The
  executor now hears drivers: it answers their claims and records their events.
  It still doesn't send commands, so nothing runs end to end yet. That's S5b,
  next.

  How it works (services/executor/receive.go)
  - On start: Start → listen sets up the LAB_EVENTS stream and a durable
    consumer named executor. Both live in NATS, so events published while the
    executor is down wait for it.
  - Claims (onClaim): the executor writes Claimed in one row-locked transaction,
    then replies.
    - go only if the saved step is claimed and not cancelled. A cancel takes the
      same lock, so whichever commits first wins.
    - If the database can't be reached, there's no reply. The driver asks again
      and never runs without a go.
  - Events (onEvent): every message gets settled one way or another, so one bad
    event can't block the stream.
    - Malformed, or not matching its subject: Term (dropped for good).
    - Applied, or about a job we don't know: Ack.
    - Database error: Nak, retried after 1s.
    - stored: Nak, retried every 30s until S6 handles it.
    - The ack goes out only after the commit (K11).
  - On close: Close drains both, so an event that's mid-apply gets acked rather
    than redelivered 30s later.

  Why this way: the alternative was a push consumer that acks on receipt. That's
  simpler, but a crash between the ack and the commit would silently lose a
  fact.

  Codex found one real problem, fixed here: a claim or event was matched by job
  id only, never by device. A misrouted job could have been recorded from
  another instrument's ledger, which breaks the invariant. applyToJob now also
  matches device_id (store.go:21). A report from the wrong device counts as an
  unknown job: its claim is aborted, and its event is acked and logged.

  What could go wrong (logged in concerns.md):
  - stored events are redelivered every 30s until S6 (K21).
  - The stream has no retention limit (K22).
  - Dropped events are only logged, with nowhere to inspect them later (K23).
  - Claims are handled one at a time, so a slow database delays all of them
    (K24).

  Evidence
  - go build, go vet and go test ./... all pass, including 6 new tests against
    an in-process NATS and a throwaway Postgres.
  - I broke four rules on purpose and the tests caught each one: no subject
    check, Nak-ing unknown jobs, replying go despite a cancel, and dropping the
    device match.
  - One of my own test bugs is worth knowing about. The impostor event first
    reused the real event's Nats-Msg-Id, and JetStream dropped the real one as a
    duplicate. So msg-id dedupe applies to anyone who publishes with that id,
    not just the original sender.

  Questions to check your understanding
  1. Why does the claim handler send no reply on a database error, instead of
     abort?
  2. An event for an unknown job is acked rather than Nak'd. When could that
     drop a fact we would have wanted, and why is that acceptable here?

  Next, S5b (send): a dispatch loop that moves each running run's ready steps to
  dispatched and sends RunCommand with retries. It also finalises runs and sets
  explicit NATS pings in both main.go files. I'll branch it from dev once PR #8
  is merged. Running acceptance.sh after that needs the stack's database reset
  with docker compose down -v, which wipes local data, so I'll ask you first.

⏺ S5b is written and tested; the branch is committed locally and not yet pushed.
  The executor now dispatches runs, sends commands to the drivers and finishes
  runs. Before I open the PR I need one decision from you.

  Blocker: the stack's database needs a reset.
  - The stack's executor_db has 30 runs and a steps schema that isn't ours
    (not_before, attempt, data_sha256, and no lifecycle or phase). I don't know
    where it came from; possibly the tmp/exp-* experiments.
  - Also, the executor and two of the driver containers aren't running.
  - To run acceptance.sh, I'd run docker compose down -v && docker compose up 
    --build -d. That permanently deletes those 30 runs and everything in local
    storage. Do you want me to go ahead, or keep anything first?
  - Expected result: about 4 of 7 checks pass. The default workflow includes a
    plate read, which stays running until S6 stores its data, so the run can't
    complete yet. That's the honest answer, not a bug.

  How it works (services/executor/send.go)
  1. Loop: dispatchLoop runs every 500ms. It also runs straight away when
     StartRun is called or an event changes a step (receive.go, via poke).
  2. Each pass (advance): read the run's steps from Postgres and ask NextSteps
     what's ready. A dispatched step counts as occupying its device. Each ready
     step gets a Dispatched fact, in the same row-locked transaction as in S2.
  3. Sending (send/command): every step still dispatched gets its RunCommand,
     with only one request per job out at a time.
     - Busy or no answer: ask again after 500ms.
     - Accepted or known: ask again after 3s, in case the claim never comes.
     - Repeats are harmless: the driver answers known and runs nothing without a
       claim.
  4. Finishing (finish): only when nothing is ready and nothing is in flight.
     - Completed if every step completed; failed if one failed.
     - finished_at is the latest ledger time.
     - The update is guarded by status = 'running', so a run can't finish twice.
  5. Pings: both main.go files now use explicit NATS pings, every 5s, with 2
     allowed to go unanswered (C1).

  Why this way: all state comes from Postgres on every pass, with no in-memory
  queue. A restarted executor just carries on, and TestDispatchSurvivesRestart
  shows it. The only thing kept in memory is when to resend, and losing it costs
  one extra harmless resend.

  What could go wrong (I'll add these to concerns.md before pushing):
  - Stuck runs: a step that's claimed but never started (driver crash), or hung,
    keeps its run running forever. That's truthful, and it's S7 or the hang
    policy.
  - Late facts after a run finishes: a late DataStored after DataLost could flip
    a step to completed while the run says failed.
  - Steps after a failure: they stay pending. They were never sent, so the
    record doesn't claim otherwise.
  - Cost per pass: every pass rescans all running runs with one query per run.
    Fine at this scale.
  - Busy devices: two runs sharing a device just retry on busy, with no
    fairness.
  - Attempt numbers: still always 1 (K2).

  Evidence
  - go build, go vet and go test ./... all pass. The executor tests also pass 3
    times in a row under -race.
  - 6 new end-to-end tests with fake drivers: run completes, busy retried, lost
    reply not run twice, failed step fails the run, restart, run waits for every
    step.
  - Mutation check: finishing a run without the in-flight check fails
    TestRunCompletesEndToEnd.
  - My first attempt at that mutation didn't compile, and my grep hid the error,
    so it looked like it passed. Nothing counted until I re-ran it properly.
  - Codex: "No new problems found."

  Questions to check your understanding
  1. After a restart the resend timing is lost and every dispatched step is sent
     again at once. Why can't that make an instrument run a job twice?
  2. Why does finish wait for in-flight steps even when another step has already
     failed?

  Created PR #10 (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ S6 is done: plate reads now complete properly, and the workflow runs end to
  end. Acceptance passes 7 of 7 on both workflows. PR #10 is open into dev:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/10

  How it works
  1. Driver (services/driver/adapter.go):
     - The plate reader's bytes exist only in what Run returns, so the driver
       now keeps them. The new run struct lets waitLedger see that Run returned
       without throwing those bytes away.
     - After finished, store uploads the bytes to runs/<job>.csv, retrying until
       storage accepts them, then publishes stored.
     - Until then the job is still the driver's current one, so the device
       answers busy. The only copy of the data is never dropped to take new
       work.
  2. Executor (services/executor/receive.go), on stored:
     - It checks the ref is where this job's data belongs. It works that out
       itself, never from the driver.
     - It reads the object back, hashes it, and applies DataStored.
     - The lifecycle from S1 decides: matching hash → completed with a data_ref;
       different hash → still running, with "does not match" in error.
     - If the object is missing or unreadable, the event is checked again after
       2s.

  Why this way: the alternative was to trust the driver, which already knows the
  bytes it uploaded. But the invariant says data claims need the stored object
  as evidence, so the executor reads it back.

  Codex findings (both P1, both deferred rather than fixed, since you asked me
  not to apply Codex fixes without asking):
  1. A verified object deleted later still shows as completed. Deferred to K34.
     A deletion goes unnoticed on every path, not just this one, and D2 says a
     verified completion is never undone. The fix is a periodic re-check that
     adds a warning.
  2. A driver restart mid-upload leaves the read running forever: the bytes are
     lost, or the upload worked but stored was never sent. Deferred to R1,
     extending K32. The record says running rather than claiming success, so it
     isn't false. But the read never resolves, so I'd make R1 the next slice. If
     you'd rather treat it as a blocker for S6, say so and I'll hold the merge.

  What could go wrong
  - Storage outage (K30): the plate reader is held for as long as storage is
    down.
  - Missing object (K31): it is checked every 2s with no end.
  - Driver restart (K32): the read is stuck, as in finding 2. This really
    happened on the stack. The S5b run run-8c366ff2ecb6 is still running,
    because rebuilding the driver threw away its bytes.
  - Redelivered stored (K33): each redelivery adds another storage check.

  Evidence
  - go build, go vet and go test ./... pass, and -race is clean.
  - New tests against the compose storage:
    - driver: data uploaded and reported; no stored while storage is down;
    - executor: hash matches, hash differs, object missing then present, ref
      pointing elsewhere.
  - Two mutations, both caught: skipping the ref check, and trusting the ledger
    hash instead of hashing the object.
  - Two mutations didn't compile at first and printed nothing; I re-ran both so
    they compiled.
  - acceptance.sh: PCR Amplification 7/7 in 12s, Triple Assay 7/7 in 6s.

  Questions to check your understanding
  1. Why does the executor work out the object key itself instead of reading the
     ref the driver sent?
  2. Why does the driver keep the device busy while storage is down, instead of
     freeing it and uploading in the background?

  Next: R1. When a driver starts, it goes through the reads its ledger shows as
  completed. If the object is there, it publishes stored; if not, data_lost.
  That fixes K32 and resolves the stuck S5b run.

  Created PR #11, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ R1 is done and open as PR #11:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/11. A restarted
  driver now re-reports how its jobs ended, so the S5b run that was stuck
  running is now failed with the true reason. Acceptance still passes 7 of 7,
  and all build, vet and test checks pass.

  How it works (services/driver/adapter.go)
  1. Snapshot first: Serve copies the ledger (earlier) before it subscribes to
     commands. Everything in that copy ended, or was cut off, under the previous
     process.
  2. Pass 1: replay publishes started and finished for every ended job, using
     the ledger's own times. A job cut off by the restart shows as hung in the
     ledger, so its step becomes unresolved instead of running forever.
  3. Pass 2: for each completed plate read, recoverData asks storage whether the
     object exists:
     - present → stored, and the executor reads it back and checks the hash, as
       in S6;
     - absent → wait until 10s after start, then ask again; if it's still absent
       → data_lost;
     - no answer → ask again. Silence is never treated as lost.
  4. Repeats are harmless: JetStream drops a repeat with the same msg id within
     its window. After that, the executor's lifecycle ignores it.

  Why this way: only a restarted driver can know its bytes are gone, because
  they were only in the old process's memory. The executor can't tell a slow
  upload from a lost one. That's why data_lost comes only from replay, and only
  for jobs in the snapshot. Otherwise a new read that's still uploading could be
  reported lost.

  Codex findings (you chose to fix both; both now have tests, and both mutations
  are caught):
  1. P1: right after a restart, a missing object might be an upload from the old
     process that hasn't landed yet. Now data_lost waits for the grace period
     and a second check.
  2. P2: a read waiting on storage held back the outcomes of later jobs. Now all
     outcomes go out in pass 1, before any storage checks.

  What could go wrong
  - Replay cost (K35, K37): the whole ledger is replayed on every start, and
    each stored read is checked again. Fine at this scale.
  - The 10s grace period (K36) is inferred, not measured. It assumes an upload
    from a dead client lands within that time or never. A /break experiment that
    kills the plate reader mid-upload would test it.
  - The snapshot rule has no test (K38). TestReplayLeavesNewJobsAlone passes
    whether or not the snapshot is taken, because replay finishes before any
    command arrives.

  Evidence
  - 8 new replay tests. Three mutations, all caught: data_lost on a storage
    error, no grace period, and a single pass.
  - -race is clean on the driver.
  - On the stack: after the plate reader restarted, it logged data of 
    run-8c366ff2ecb6/read_plate/1 was never stored and is gone. That run is now
    failed with "instrument completed the read, but its data was lost before
    storage". Its finished_at is the ledger time. The four completed runs stayed
    completed.

  Questions to check your understanding
  1. Why must replay use the ledger as it was before the driver subscribed to
     commands?
  2. Storage is down when the plate reader restarts. What does the record say
     about its last read, and why is that true?

  What's left before submission: S7 (finding steps that were claimed but never
  started, K19/K25), cancel, the hang/retry policy (needs /decide), GraphQL,
  /break chaos experiments, then NOTES.md and exporting the AI conversations to
  ai/. My suggestion for next is the hang/retry decision. Hung steps currently
  stay unresolved indefinitely, and the chaos runs will hit that first.

❯ the 10 second grace period doesn't guarantee that a delayed upload won't      
  succeed later. Make sure we don't incorrectly finalize a run as failed while  
  the outcome is still uncertain.                                               

⏺ You're right. A timeout can only make a late upload unlikely, never
  impossible. Worse, today a data_lost makes the step failed straight away, and
  the run gets finalised as failed. If the late upload then lands, the record
  has claimed something storage contradicts (that's K26).

  What would make it certain is having storage decide which write counts.
  Postgres already does that for claim versus cancel. If every write to the key
  is "create only if absent" (If-None-Match: *), then:
  - the restarted driver, before saying data_lost, writes a tombstone to the key
    the same way;
  - whichever write lands first wins for good, and a late upload after a
    tombstone is refused;
  - so "lost" becomes a fact, not a guess.

  That only works if SeaweedFS honours conditional PUTs. I'll measure that
  before proposing it:

⏺ Fixed. A read reported as lost no longer finishes its run as failed. It stays
  unresolved until storage shows the data or someone decides. PR #11 is updated:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/11

  Why there's no time-based fix. I tested whether storage could reject a late
  upload, using "create only if absent" writes. SeaweedFS 3.62 ignores that
  header: a second conditional PUT returned 200 and overwrote the first. So
  nothing can make "lost" final, and a timeout of any length can still be
  followed by the upload landing.

  What changed
  - Lifecycle (services/executor/lifecycle.go):
    - DataLost now makes a read unresolved instead of failed. The API shows
      running, and error says "driver lost the data before it was confirmed
      stored; an earlier upload may still appear in storage".
    - The run counts an unresolved step as still in progress, so it can't
      finish.
    - This replaces part of D2, which made DataLost final.
  - Executor recheck (recheckLoop in services/executor/receive.go):
    - Every 10s, the executor reads the stored object for each read reported
      lost. The driver that sent the upload is dead, so nobody else will report
      it if it lands.
    - If the object matches the ledger's hash, the read and its run complete.
      Late facts win, without any status moving backwards.
  - Driver (services/driver/adapter.go): the grace period stays, but only to
    avoid a needless data_lost report. Correctness no longer depends on it, and
    the comments say so.
  - D3 in docs/decisions.md records the choice, the alternatives (fail after a
    grace period, conditional writes, per-process keys) and the measurement.

  What's still open: nothing ends a read whose data really is gone, so that run
  stays running indefinitely (K39). Ending it has to be an explicit decision:
  re-run the read (the hang/retry /decide) or an operator declaring it lost
  (K6). I'd fold this into the hang/retry decision next, since hung steps have
  the same problem.

  Evidence
  - Unit test: TestLostDataKeepsTheRunOpenUntilStorageSettlesIt. A fake driver
    reports the data lost, and the run must stay running. After a late PUT it
    must complete. It catches both "lost means failed" and a disabled recheck.
  - Live on the stack (logged as chaos experiment C2):
    a. I started a run and stopped storage while the plate read was running.
    b. I restarted the plate reader after the read completed, so the bytes were
       lost, then started storage again.
    c. The restarted driver reported the data lost. The read showed as running
       with the "may still appear" error, and the run stayed running.
    d. I then played the dead driver's late upload: the instrument's data
       depends only on the job id, so I rebuilt the bytes (sha 652dd17c…, equal
       to the ledger's) and PUT them.
    e. Within 10s the run was completed, with the ledger's finished_at and
       data_ref set.
  - acceptance.sh passed 7/7 afterwards. All tests pass, including under -race,

  Redoing it with a proper YAML parse:

⏺ The measurement settles one thing. Under a given seed, a step's first attempt
  has the same fate in every run. For example, with LH_HANG_PCT=30 and seed 0,
  fill_sample_plate hangs on attempts 0 and 1 of every PCR run; the first try
  that gets through is attempt 2. Without retries, that workflow can never
  complete under that setting. (The table above rolls against one 30% threshold.
  With fail and hang both set, the low numbers split into "fail" first, then
  "hang".)

  Question

  What ends a step whose attempt can't succeed as it stands, and when do we
  start a new attempt? Today nothing ends it:
  - hung: the ledger says so after 30s, but the step stays unresolved forever;
  - data lost: unresolved by D3;
  - failed: final, never retried.

  This matters for truth because a step marked running forever after the
  instrument has freed itself is a claim that's no longer true. And a careless
  retry can physically repeat liquid handling.

  Facts

  - Hang (README:224, instrument.go:192-199): the instrument frees itself after
    HANG_DURATION and the ledger says hung. Our driver then publishes
    finished(hung) (unit test). After that, nothing more will ever be learned 
    about that attempt. Inferred from code.
  - Retryable failures (docker-compose.yml): FAILURES_RETRYABLE is false for the
    liquid handler and true for the incubator and plate reader. The flag comes
    back only in Run's returned Outcome, not the ledger, so the driver would
    have to forward it. Inferred.
  - Rolls: a retry rolls a new fate, because attempt counts this run's earlier
    executions of the step. Measured above.
  - Acceptance check 5 fails whenever a step runs twice, so any retry fails it
    under faults. With faults off (the happy path), nothing retries. This is K5.

  Alternatives

  A. No automatic retry; an operator decides.
  - Hung and lost steps stay unresolved. GraphQL gets retryStep and abandonStep
    mutations.
  - Rests on someone watching, and on "repeating any lab operation is unsafe".
    That holds for liquid handling but not for a plate read.
  - Under a fault seed, runs never finish on their own (see the measurement).

  B. Automatic, bounded, with a per-device "safe to repeat" setting. 
  Recommended.
  - Each device declares repeat_safe in config: plate reader yes, incubator yes,
    liquid handler no.
  - When a step gets a new attempt:
    - failed, if the instrument said Retryable (forwarded in finished, an
      additive field);
    - hung, only on a repeat-safe device, and only after finished(hung) arrives.
      The device is free by then, so no timer or clock is involved;
    - data_lost, on the plate reader: a re-read.
  - Limit: 3 attempts in total. When they run out, or the device isn't safe to
    repeat, the step is failed with the reason, e.g. "instrument hung; outcome
    unknown; not repeated: liquid handling is not safe to repeat".
  - History: the step keeps every attempt (fixes K2). Old attempts' late facts
    are still recorded, found by parsing job_id = run/step/attempt. The step's
    status is its latest attempt. A late upload for attempt 1 is recorded but
    doesn't change the attempt that counts.
  - Cancel: a new attempt goes through dispatch and claim, so a cancel still
    wins.
  - Run: finishes completed or failed only when no attempt is in flight (D3
    unchanged).
  - Rests on repeat-safety being knowable per device. That's true of this
    simulator's config, but it's a guess about real equipment, e.g. whether
    re-incubating is harmless.

  C. Retry only on the instrument's own Retryable flag.
  - No device setting. It can't decide about hangs, because a hang returns no
    Outcome, so it ends up as A (never retry hangs) or as an unsafe B (always
    retry them).

  One conflict to flag

  In S1 you said "a hung job doesn't necessarily mean failed", and today a hung
  step stays running. But CLAUDE.md says "failed with the reason once we can't
  [find out]", and once the ledger says hung we can't find out any more. B
  proposes failed with error "outcome unknown". That status means "this step did
  not achieve its result", not "the instrument did nothing", and it's less
  false than running forever after the instrument has stopped. It's your call.

  What would change my mind: if repeat-safety can't be stated honestly per
  device, then A. If check 5 must hold under faults, then A.

⏺ I've written the explainer and opened it in your browser:
  tmp/d4-hang-retry.html. Here's the reasoning behind it.

  1. What's actually broken

  Three kinds of step never end today:
  - Hung: the instrument freezes, frees itself after 30s and writes hung in its
    ledger. Our step then shows running forever.
  - Data lost: under D3 the read stays unresolved in case a late upload lands,
    and nothing ever ends it.
  - Failed: this one does end, but it's never retried, even when the instrument
    itself said the failure is retryable.

  "Claimed but never started" is a different problem. Nothing physical happened,
  so the fix is to resend the same job (reconcile, S7), not to start a new
  attempt.

  2. Why a retry is both necessary and dangerous

  A retry is a new job id (run/step/2). That's exactly what lets it run again,
  because the driver only refuses repeats of the same id. It's also what makes
  it risky: a hung liquid handler may have dispensed, and a retry would dispense
  twice.

  The measurement makes retries necessary. The instrument picks a fault from
  seed:device:step:attempt, with no run id in it. Under one seed, the first try
  of a step has the same fate in every run. With LH_HANG_PCT=30,
  fill_sample_plate hangs on every PCR run's first and second try. Without
  retries that workflow can never complete under that setting. It's also the
  device that's least safe to repeat, so the two goals pull against each other
  right there.

  3. The options in one line each

  - A, an operator decides: safe, but under faults runs never end on their own.
  - B, automatic with a per-device "safe to repeat" flag:
    - repeat retryable failures, hangs (only after finished(hung) arrives) and
      lost reads;
    - up to 3 attempts, then failed with the reason.
    - There's no timer: the retry is triggered by the instrument's own report
      that it gave up, so attempt 2 is never sent to a frozen instrument.
  - C, the instrument's flag only: can't decide about hangs, because a hang
    returns no flag.

  The page has a table with every scenario under every option, and a timeline of
  a hung incubation retried under B (sections 6 and 7).

  4. What changes in the record (section 8)

  A step keeps a list of attempts, and its status comes from the latest one.
  That fixes K2, where a step holds only one attempt.
  - The REST API, whose shape is fixed, shows the latest attempt only. GraphQL
    would show the full history.
  - Late events for an old attempt still find their step, because the job id is
    parsed to locate it.
  - The ledger shows two executions and the record shows two attempts, so they
    agree. Acceptance check 5 will still say "ran twice" under faults, which is
    the honest result.

  5. The question you were asked (section 9)

  When a hang won't be repeated, should the step be failed with "outcome
  unknown", or running until someone acts?
  - For failed: CLAUDE.md says "failed with the reason once we can't find out",
    and after hung nobody can find out anything more. running would claim
    something is still happening after the instrument has stopped.
  - For running: it keeps your S1 point that hung isn't necessarily failed. The
    difference from D3 is that a lost read can still be found later; a hang
    can't.

  You're deciding three things (section 11):
  1. the policy (A, B or C);
  2. the final status of a hang that won't be repeated;
  3. if B, which devices are safe to repeat (I proposed the plate reader and
     incubator) and the attempt limit (I proposed 3).

  Which part would you like to go through in more detail, or do you have
  questions about the page itself?

✻ Churned for 1m 58s · done Monday 13:39

※ recap: We're building a lab-automation executor whose run record stays true, and we're deciding 
  how stuck steps (hangs, lost data, failures) should be retried or ended. Next: read 
  tmp/d4-hang-retry.html and pick option A, B or C plus how a final hang is shown.

❯ A retryable failure, a hung instrument, and lost plate-reader data aren't     
  necessarily the same situation. Especially with D3, lost data might still     
  arrive later.                                                                 
  Any thing we should do?                                                       

⏺ Yes. My draft treated all three as one "retry trigger", and that hides two
  real differences: whether the first attempt is finished, and whether repeating
  it is physically safe.

  Retryable failure
  Is attempt 1 finished?: yes, the ledger says failed
  What do we know physically?: it failed, and the instrument says retrying is
  sensible
  Is repeating safe?: yes, on the instrument's own word
  Can attempt 1 still change?: no
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Hung
  Is attempt 1 finished?: yes, the ledger says hung
  What do we know physically?: unknown: it may have done some or all of the work
  Is repeating safe?: only if the device's operation is repeat-safe
  Can attempt 1 still change?: no
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Data lost
  Is attempt 1 finished?: no (D3)
  What do we know physically?: the read happened; only the data is missing
  Is repeating safe?: the plate reader only looks (a guess for real readers)
  Can attempt 1 still change?: yes: a late upload can still land

  The third row breaks the rule in my draft, "the step's status comes from its
  latest attempt":
  - Wrong failure: attempt 1 (lost) is still open while attempt 2 runs. If
    attempt 2 fails and the retries run out, the draft marks the step failed,
    even though attempt 1's upload could still land. That's exactly what D3
    forbids.
  - Run never ends: if every open attempt kept the run open, a re-read that
    succeeded would still leave the run stuck behind attempt 1, which may never
    settle. The re-read would buy nothing.

  What I'd change

  1. Decide a step from all of its attempts, not the latest one.
  - completed once any attempt is verified complete. If several are, the first
    verified one supplies data_ref, which is D2: a verified completion is never
    undone. Later ones are kept in the history.
  - failed only when every attempt is finished, none completed, and no further
    attempt will be made.
  - Otherwise it stays in progress (running), including while any attempt could
    still produce data.

  Under this rule a lost read can never make a step falsely failed, and a
  successful re-read lets the run finish. Attempt 1 stays in the history as
  "data lost; may still appear". If its upload lands later, it's recorded as
  attempt 1's data and doesn't replace the result.

  2. Give each cause its own trigger and limit.
  - Retryable failure: retry straight away. The instrument is free and has said
    it's sensible.
  - Hung: retry after finished(hung), and only on a repeat-safe device.
    Otherwise mark it failed with "outcome unknown", if you agree with that
    part.
  - Data lost: re-read right away, alongside the attempt that's still open.
    Don't wait for the late upload: D3 showed there's no limit on how long that
    wait could be. The driver has already waited its 10s grace before reporting
    data_lost.

  3. Be honest that a re-read is a different measurement.
  - A re-read happens later, so the plate may have changed. The step's data_ref
    and times must come from the attempt whose data counts, not from attempt 1.
  - In this simulator the data depends on the job id, so the two reads really do
    differ. Whether a delayed re-read is acceptable in a real lab is a
    scientific question, a guess on my part. That's an argument for a per-device
    "re-read on data loss" setting, separate from "safe to repeat after a
    hang".

  4. What's still left. If every re-read also loses its data, the step stays
  running: some upload could still land, so D3 still applies. K39 shrinks to
  that rare case, which an operator's "abandon" would cover.

  Next

  - These points change option B, so I'd record them in D4 if you choose B.
  - I'd make an explainer V2 (tmp/d4-hang-retry-v2.html) rather than editing the
    current page, as with D1. It would show the table above, the "all attempts"
    rule with a timeline of a lost read and its re-read, and the case where the
    re-read fails while attempt 1 is still open.

  Shall I make V2, or do you want to settle the policy questions first?

  The V2 explainer (tmp/d4-hang-retry-v2.html, open in your browser; V1 is
  unchanged):
  - A "What changed from v1" box at the top, and each changed section is tagged.
  - §3 is new: the three causes are told apart by two questions: is attempt 1
    finished, and is repeating it safe? Each cause gets its own trigger.
  - §6 is the corrected rule: a step is decided by all its attempts, with the
    two cases that show why "latest attempt" was wrong.
  - §8 is new: a timeline of a lost read, its re-read, and attempt 1's late
    upload being recorded without replacing the result.
  - §12 says what was decided.

  D4 as recorded (docs/decisions.md):
  - Triggers:
    - retryable failure: a new attempt straight away;
    - hang: a new attempt after finished(hung), only on a device that's safe to
      repeat; otherwise failed with "outcome unknown";
    - lost data: a re-read straight away, while attempt 1 stays open (D3).
  - At most 3 attempts. A cancel still wins over a retry.
  - A step is completed once any attempt is verified. The first verified attempt
    supplies data_ref, as D2 requires.
  - A step is failed only when every attempt is finished, none completed, and no
    further attempt will be made. An attempt that's still open doesn't hold the
    run back once another attempt of the same step has completed.
  - Settings I filled in from my proposal, since you didn't name them:
    - the plate reader and the incubator are safe to repeat; the liquid handler
      isn't;
    - the plate reader re-reads on data loss;
    - a hang that won't be repeated ends failed with "outcome unknown".

    Say if you want any of these changed. They're per-device settings, not code.
  - Alternatives rejected: A, C, the V1 draft (after your review), and leaving a
    final hang as running. Each one has its reason.
  - Concerns updated: K2 (a step keeps only one attempt) will be fixed by D4.
    K39 (a lost read that never ends) is narrowed to the case where every
    re-read also fails. K19 stays with reconcile, S7: it needs the same job
    resent, not a new attempt.

  One thing worth knowing from the measured fault table: with LH_HANG_PCT=30,
  fill_sample_plate hangs on the first try of every PCR run. The liquid handler
  isn't safe to repeat, so under D4 every one of those runs ends failed with
  "outcome unknown". That's the policy putting physical safety ahead of the run
  finishing. It will also be the first prediction to check in the /break
  experiment.

  Next, once #12 is merged, D4 is built in four slices:
  1. Protocol: add the retryable field to finished.
  2. Lifecycle: a list of attempts per step and the all-attempts rule. This is
     the big one.
  3. Store: find a step by parsing the job id, so late events for old attempts
     still land.
  4. Policy: the per-device settings, and deciding when a retry is due.

  After that comes a /break run with hang faults, predicted from the table
  first.

❯ A retryable failure, a hung instrument, and lost plate-reader data aren't     
  necessarily the same situation. Especially with D3, lost data might still     
  arrive later.                                                                 
                                                                                
  Could we review the proposed retry rules from this perspective and suggest a  
  safe, practical policy?                                                       

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I re-read D4 against the code with one question for each cause: did the
  physical work happen, can more evidence still arrive, and who says repeating
  it is safe? Most of D4 holds up. I'd change three things before PR #12 merges.

  How the three causes differ

  Did the work happen?
  Retryable failure: Yes. It ran its full duration, then errored
  (instrument.go:206-212)
  Hung: Unknown. Nothing is reported (instrument.go:198-203)
  Data lost: Yes. The ledger says completed and has the sha
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Can more evidence arrive?
  Retryable failure: No, the ledger is final
  Hung: No, once finished(hung) is in
  Data lost: Yes. A late upload can still land (D3)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Who says a repeat is safe?
  Retryable failure: The instrument (its Retryable flag)
  Hung: Nobody. It can only be a per-device setting
  Data lost: The device setting. A re-read is a new measurement
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  D4's trigger
  Retryable failure: Retry at once
  Hung: Retry after finished(hung), only if repeat_safe
  Data lost: Re-read at once; attempt 1 stays open

  Parts of D4 that are fine as they are:
  - The hang trigger needs no timer. The driver reads the ledger, not Run's
    return (adapter.go:41). So finished(hung) arrives once the instrument frees
    itself after HANG_DURATION (30s), and a retry never goes to an instrument
    that's still busy. (inferred)
  - Attempts can't overwrite each other's data. The data key is
    runs/<job_id>.csv and the job id includes the attempt number. A late upload
    from attempt 1 lands under its own key.
  - The all-attempts rule handles a late upload correctly. Whichever attempt is
    verified first supplies data_ref, and nothing replaces it later (D2).

  What I'd change

  1. Make one rule explicit: a new attempt needs a positive claim that repeating
  is safe. If there's no claim, don't retry.
  - Each cause has its own source for that claim:
    - retryable failure: the instrument's flag;
    - hang: the device's repeat_safe setting;
    - data lost: the device's reread_on_data_loss setting.
  - Why this matters: Retryable exists only in Run's return, not in the ledger
    (inferred, instrument.go:211). Suppose the driver restarts before it reports
    a failure. Replay then sends finished(failed) without the flag. D4 doesn't
    say what happens next. Under this rule the answer is clear and safe: no
    retry, the step is failed.
  - The cost: the same physical failure can end differently depending on whether
    the driver restarted. I think that's acceptable, because the difference
    always leans toward not repeating.

  2. Don't treat the incubator as safe to repeat after a hang. This is the one
  default I'd reverse.
  - A hung incubation may have run for part of its time, all of it, or none.
    Repeating it adds incubation time of unknown length to the same plate.
  - Reading a plate again is harmless. Incubating it again changes the sample.
  - Calling the incubator repeat-safe was my guess, and it was the weakest claim
    in D4.
  - The cost (measured roll): with INC_HANG_PCT=30 and seed 0, Triple Assay's
    incubate hangs on its first attempt in every run. Every such run would end
    failed: "instrument hung; outcome unknown; not repeated". That's the honest
    outcome. It's also the same trade we already accepted for the liquid
    handler.
  - The incubator would still retry after a retryable failure, because there the
    instrument itself vouches for the repeat.

  3. Add one operator escape hatch for the case D4 leaves open.
  - The case: a read loses its data, and every re-read fails or also loses its
    data. Attempt 1 is still open, so the step stays running forever (K39).
  - That's true, but not useful. Nobody can close the run.
  - The fix: a GraphQL abandonStep(run, step) that marks the step failed, with
    the reason "abandoned by operator; a late upload may still appear".
  - Limits:
    - It's only allowed while the step is still running with no verified
      attempt, so it can never undo a completion (D2).
    - A late upload that lands afterwards still goes into the attempt history.
  - We have to build GraphQL anyway, so this is one mutation. It's option A,
    used only where B can't decide.

  Considered and not recommended: waiting for one recheck (10s) before
  re-reading after a data loss. That would save a read if attempt 1's upload
  lands quickly, but there's no correctness reason for it, and it adds a timer.
  I'd keep "re-read at once".

  Policy with these changes

  - Failed, with the flag set: retry at once.
  - Failed, without the flag: failed.
  - Hung on a repeat-safe device (plate reader only): retry after
    finished(hung).
  - Hung on any other device: failed, outcome unknown.
  - Data lost: re-read at once while attempt 1 stays open.
    - The first verified attempt decides the step.
    - If no attempt is ever verified, an operator can abandon the step.
  - Limit: 3 attempts per step in total. A cancel always wins.

  All three changes are edits to D4's text, so they can go into PR #12 before
  you merge it. Do you want all three, or a subset? Change 2 is the one most
  worth your own judgement: it trades Triple Assay finishing under hang faults
  for not over-incubating a plate.

⏺ I've updated D4 with changes 1 and 2, made its cancel rules match D1, and
  recorded change 3 as deferred. The commit is aed3326, pushed to PR #12, and I
  updated the PR description to match. Your .gitignore change stays out of the
  PR.

  Change 1, the safe-repeat rule. D4 now opens with it: a new attempt needs a
  positive claim that repeating is safe, and with no claim there's no retry.
  - Each cause has one source for that claim:
    - failure: the instrument's Retryable flag;
    - hang: the device's repeat_safe setting;
    - data lost: the device's reread_on_data_loss setting.
  - After a driver restart, replay sends finished(failed) without the flag, so
    that step is failed, not retried.
  - So the same failure can end differently around a restart. When it does, it
    always ends on the side of not repeating.

  Change 2, the incubator isn't repeat-safe. Only the plate reader is now safe
  to repeat after a hang.
  - The incubator still retries a failure that the instrument itself marks as
    retryable.
  - The cost is written into D4: at INC_HANG_PCT=30 with seed 0, every Triple
    Assay run ends failed with "outcome unknown; not repeated".
  - Your first choice (incubator repeat-safe) is listed under rejected
    alternatives with the reason.

  Change 3, deferred. It's recorded as a "Deferred" item in D4, and K39 now
  points to it.
  - An operator abandonStep would mark the step failed, with the reason
    "abandoned by operator; a late upload may still appear".
  - It would only be allowed while the step is running with no verified attempt.

  Cancel across attempts, kept consistent with D1. D1 says a cancel stops what
  hasn't started, and once a step is claimed only its evidence says how it
  ended. Applied to attempts:
  - A cancel is always recorded, whatever state the step is in, and no new 
    attempt is dispatched after it. A retry and a cancel take the same row lock,
    so one of them is always first, just as claim and cancel are in D1.
  - An attempt that's been dispatched but not claimed: it's cancelled, and
    exactly one of the claim and the cancel wins.
  - An attempt that's already claimed: it isn't stopped, and its late facts are
    still recorded.
  - The step's status: it's cancelled only if no attempt was ever claimed.
    Otherwise its attempts decide it as usual; with no further attempts allowed,
    that ends as failed, "…; not retried: cancelled", unless an attempt
    completed. A cancel never hides an attempt that ran, and never undoes a
    completion (D2).
  - A read whose data was lost before the cancel: it isn't re-read, and its
    attempt stays open until the upload appears (D3).

  New concern K42. finish has no rule yet for a run with a cancelled step: today
  such a run would never finish. D4 only decides steps, so whether the run ends
  cancelled, or failed when a claimed attempt failed, is left to the cancel
  slice.

  A question worth being able to answer: run attempt 1 of a step, and it hangs
  on the incubator. A cancel then arrives before the hang is reported. What does
  the step end as, and why not cancelled?

⏺ The D4 protocol slice is built and verified on branch
  slice/d4-protocol-retryable, in two commits. It's 99 lines changed, and I'll
  open the PR once you're happy with it.

  What it does: when a step fails, its finished event now says whether the
  instrument considers the failure safe to repeat. Nothing acts on that yet; the
  lifecycle and policy slices will.

  How it works

  1. Wire format (protocol.go:117-119): finished gets an optional retryable
     field. When it's absent, the failure is not retryable.
     - It doesn't break older code: Decode doesn't reject unknown fields, so an
       older executor just ignores it, and the protocol version stays at 1.
  2. Driver (adapter.go:131-143): the driver learns that a job ended by polling
     the ledger, every 50ms.
     - The flag isn't in the ledger. It's only in Run's return value, and Run
       returns just after the ledger records the end.
     - So on a failure, the driver now waits for Run to return before it
       publishes finished, and copies the flag across.
     - On a hang it doesn't wait, because a hung Run never returns.
  3. Replay: replay reads only the ledger, so a failure reported after a driver
     restart never carries the flag. That's D4 change 1 working as intended: a
     failure we heard about only after a restart is never repeated.
  4. Executor (receive.go:186, lifecycle.go:136): the executor stores the flag
     with the step. It keeps it only on a failure, and only from the first
     finished report. A later replay without the flag doesn't clear it.

  Why this way

  - The alternative was a per-device "failures are retryable" setting in the
    executor. That would copy the instrument's config, and the two copies could
    drift apart.
  - D4 says the instrument is the authority on repeating a failure, so its own
    answer is what gets carried.

  What could go wrong

  - The driver stops between the ledger's end and Run returning (K43): no
    finished is published live. Replay then reports the failure without the
    flag, so the step fails instead of retrying. The window is microseconds, and
    it errs on the side of not repeating.
  - Someone later reads the result without waiting (K44): that mutant survived
    the plain test suite, because the 50ms ledger poll almost always lets Run
    return first. Only go test -race caught it (measured). K44 proposes adding
    -race to every slice's verify step.

  Evidence (measured)

  - Tests: written first; all three failed for the right reason.
    - Driver: the flag wasn't forwarded.
    - Executor: the step's Retryable came back false. Postgres was down at
      first, so this test was skipped; I started Postgres and re-ran it to see
      it fail.
    - Lifecycle: the first report wins, and the flag is kept only on a failure.
  - Suites: go build, go vet and go test ./... pass, and go test -race ./...
    passes with no races.
  - Mutation checks: 4 mutants, all of which compiled. Three were caught by the
    plain tests, and the fourth only under -race.
  - Acceptance: after rebuilding the stack (volumes kept), all 7 checks passed.
  - Live check on the stack:
    - With liquid-handler failures forced, prepare was recorded failed with
      Retryable false.
    - With incubator failures forced, incubate was recorded failed with
      Retryable true.
    - Both match each device's FAILURES_RETRYABLE. The stack is back on its
      default settings.

  Codex

  No problems found. It couldn't run the tests in its read-only sandbox, so its
  review covers the code only.

  Questions

  1. Why does the driver wait for Run to return on a failure, but not on a hang?
  2. A liquid-handler failure is replayed after a restart, and an incubator
     failure is replayed the same way. Under D4, does either get retried?

  Shall I push and open the PR into dev?

⏺ Before writing code I need your OK on the framing. This slice changes how a
  step's state is modelled, and it includes one visible behaviour change.

  Behaviour, as seen from outside:
  - A step keeps every attempt it has made.
  - Its API status comes from all of those attempts together:
    - completed once any attempt is verified complete;
    - failed only once every attempt has ended, none completed, and the executor
      has recorded that no further attempt will be made;
    - running otherwise.
  - Visible change: a hung step now ends failed, with the error "instrument
    hung; outcome unknown; not repeated". Today it stays running for ever.

  The check that proves it:
  - Table tests in lifecycle_test.go for the all-attempts rule, cancel across
    attempts, and which attempt decides.
  - A send_test.go test where a hung step ends its run as failed, with that
    reason.
  - The existing suite, go test -race, and acceptance.sh.

  Depends on: D4 (all-attempts rule, cancel per D1, attempt history), D2 (a
  completion is never undone), D3 (a lost read stays open), D1 (claim and cancel
  take the same lock).

  Four design choices, three of them not spelled out in D4 itself:
  1. "No further attempt" is a recorded fact, GaveUp{reason}, which the dispatch
     loop writes under the step's row lock. The alternative was working it out
     from config whenever the step is read. A recorded fact survives a restart,
     is ordered against a cancel, and stores the reason. A cancel also counts as
     "no further attempt".
  2. The attempt that decides a completed step is pinned when it first verifies.
     The alternative is to always use the lowest-numbered completed attempt.
     But then a late upload from attempt 1 could swap data_ref away from attempt
     2 after the step was already shown as completed, which D2 forbids.
  3. The decider in this slice never retries. Every attempt that ends gets
     GaveUp. That's why hangs become failed now, on every device, the plate
     reader included. The policy slice adds real retries, starting with the
     plate reader after a hang.
  4. The stored step format changes shape (K10). I'll add a version number, and
     the executor will refuse to load a step stored in the old format with a
     clear error, rather than misread it. Misreading is dangerous: an old step
     would look pending and get dispatched again.

  Size: about 170–190 lines of non-test code, slightly over the 150-line guide.
  I'm proposing not to split it:
  - A model with attempts but no decider would leave failed steps running for
    ever.
  - The format version only exists because of this change of shape.
  - The store lookup for late events from older attempts stays out. Until the
    policy slice adds retries there's only ever attempt 1, so it isn't needed
    yet.

  One thing I need from you: the local stack's Postgres holds steps in the old
  format. To run acceptance afterwards I'd reset it with docker compose down -v,
  which deletes all local runs. Is that OK, or should I write a converter for
  old rows instead?

⏺ The lifecycle slice is built, tested and committed on branch
  slice/d4-lifecycle-attempts (c9cd8e0, plus 20bdcf1 for the concerns). It's
  ready for your review; I'll open the PR when you say.

  How it works

  The model (lifecycle.go):
  - A Step is now a list of Attempts plus three step-wide intents:
    - Cancelled: a cancel was recorded;
    - GaveUp: no further attempt will be made, and why;
    - Decided: which attempt completed the step.
  - All the evidence fields (outcome, times, sha, checks, lost) moved into
    Attempt.
  - Routing: Apply (:137) sends each evidence fact to the attempt with the same
    job id. Facts about an earlier attempt are kept as that attempt's history.

  The rule (Phase, :276), checked in this order:
  1. Decided is set: completed. The exception is two different outcomes reported
     for the deciding job; then the step is unresolved, with no data_ref.
  2. No attempt ever ran, and there's a cancel: cancelled.
  3. Some attempt is still open: the step takes that attempt's phase.
     - "Open" means not ended and not cancelled. A lost read counts as open
       (D3).
     - A later attempt that's been sent but not claimed is shown as the new
       phase retrying (API running), so the step never goes back to pending.
  4. Every attempt has ended: failed if GaveUp or a cancel is recorded.
     Otherwise it's the new phase ended (API running): waiting for the retry
     decision.

  Pinning (:191): Decided is set once, by the first attempt to verify, and never
  changes after that. So a late upload from attempt 1 can't replace attempt 2's
  data_ref (D2).

  Guards:
  - New attempts (mayAttempt, :202): only the next number, and only when nothing
    is in flight. A lost read doesn't count as in flight. No new attempt is
    allowed after a cancel, a give-up, or a completion.
  - GaveUp (:155): accepted only in the ended phase, so it can never fail a step
    whose lost read could still land.
  - Cancel (:147): always recorded. It stops the current attempt only if that
    attempt hasn't run yet (D1).

  The stand-in decider (send.go:113): the dispatch loop records GaveUp{"not 
  repeated"} on every ended step. The policy slice will replace it with real
  retries.

  Format version: the stored step now carries V: 2. decodeStep (store.go:91)
  refuses any other version with a hint to reset local data, instead of
  misreading an old step as pending. That settles K10.

  Why this way

  - Recording "no further attempt" as a fact survives a restart, is ordered
    against a cancel under the row lock, and keeps the reason. Working it out
    from config on every read would have none of those properties.
  - The ended phase makes "waiting for a decision" something you can see in the
    record, rather than a gap between two other states.

  What could go wrong

  - K45, must be fixed before the policy slice: driver events still find their
    step by the job_id column, which holds only the latest attempt's job. An
    event for an earlier attempt would be dropped as unknown. Harmless today,
    because nothing creates attempt 2. The lost-read recheck is our own
    read-back, so it already goes by step, not by job.
  - K47: command always sends attempt 1. That's for the policy slice to fix.
  - K48: until the policy slice, a plate-reader hang also ends failed, even
    though D4 makes the plate reader safe to repeat.
  - K46: a conflicting report after a give-up moves a step from failed back to
    unresolved. A conflict always showed like this before D4 too; the run,
    already finished, isn't reopened.
  - K49: the lost-read recheck now decodes every unresolved step, not only lost
    ones. Fine at this scale.
  - Size: +318 / −93 non-test lines, above my 170–190 estimate. About 90 of
    those lines are existing methods moved from Step to Attempt.

  Evidence (measured)

  - New tests: 28 cases in the attempts table, plus the times test, the
    old-format refusal and TestHungStepFailsItsRun. They first failed to build
    because the model didn't exist yet. The conflict-on-the-deciding-read case
    was written first and failed (it found a data_ref leak), then passed after
    the fix.
  - Suites: go build, go vet and go test -race ./... all pass, with no races.
  - Mutation checks: 13 mutants, all of which compiled. 10 were caught at first.
    The other 3 needed two new cases (a give-up while an attempt is in flight;
    a conflict after giving up), and now all 13 are caught.
  - Acceptance: after docker compose down -v (as you approved) and a rebuild,
    both workflows passed all 7 checks.
  - Live hang: with INC_HANG_PCT=100 and HANG_DURATION=5s, the ledger showed
    incubate/1 as hung. The record showed failed, "instrument hung; outcome
    unknown; not repeated", with the ledger's times; report stayed pending; the
    run ended failed. Defaults are restored.
  - Codex: no problems found. It couldn't build in its sandbox, so its review
    was static only.

  Questions

  1. A read loses its data, the re-read then fails, and the dispatch loop tries
     to record GaveUp. Why is the step still running, and what would it take to
     end it?
  2. Attempt 2 verifies at 10:00, and attempt 1's late upload verifies at 10:01.
     Which data_ref does REST show? Why would "the lowest-numbered verified
     attempt" be wrong?

  Shall I push and open the PR?
