⏺ The K45 store fix is built, tested and committed on branch
  slice/store-job-lookup: cfe1c7c is the fix, and efde48f marks K45 and K2 done.
  It's ready for your review.

  What changed: a claim or event from a driver now reaches whichever attempt
  it's about, not only the step's latest one.

  How it works

  1. protocol.ParseJobID (protocol.go:54) splits run/step/attempt back into its
     parts.
     - The run id is everything before the first /, and the attempt is the
       number after the last /. So a step name containing / still parses.
     - A missing part, a non-number, or attempt 0 is rejected.
  2. applyToJob (store.go:29) used to find the step by the job_id column. Now it
     finds it by run_id, name and device_id, taken from the parsed id.
     - Only the step's assigned device still counts.
     - The job_id column holds only the latest attempt. Driver lookups no longer
       use it; only the dispatch loop does, to know which job to send.
  3. apply (store.go:62): once the step is loaded under its row lock, the job
     has to be one of its attempts. If it isn't, the result is ErrUnknownJob, as
     before, so an unknown attempt is still answered and acked as unknown.

  Why this way

  - The job id already carries the run and the step: JobID builds it from them.
  - The alternative was a separate table of attempts (job_id → step). That would
    need a schema change and a second write on every dispatch, to learn what
    the id itself already says.

  What could go wrong

  - A late claim for an earlier attempt: that attempt is either already claimed,
    or was cancelled before its claim. If it was cancelled, Claimed is refused,
    so the driver gets abort.
  - A late finished for an earlier attempt: it's added to that attempt's history
    only. Decided stays pinned, so a step that has already completed doesn't
    change.
  - A job id made up to look like a real one: it has to match an existing run
    and step, come from that step's device, and name an attempt the step
    actually has.

  Evidence (measured)

  - New tests:
    - TestStoreFindsEarlierAttempts: attempt 1's late upload, arriving after
      attempt 2 was sent, decides the step and supplies data_ref. It failed
      first with unknown job, which is K45 reproduced.
    - TestStoreUnknownAttempts: covers an unknown attempt number, an id that
      isn't a job id, an unknown step, and the wrong device.
    - TestParseJobID: 7 cases.
  - Suites: go build, go vet and go test -race ./... all pass.
  - Mutation checks: 6 mutants, all of which compiled, and all 6 were caught.
    The old lookup by the job_id column didn't compile at first, so I rewrote
    that mutant so it does. Caught: the old lookup, removing the device check,
    removing the attempt check, and three changes to the parsing.
  - Acceptance: after a rebuild (no reset needed), both workflows passed all 7
    checks.
  - Codex: no findings. The review was static only.

  Questions

  1. A driver sends a claim for run-x/read_plate/3, but that step only has two
     attempts. What does the driver get back, and why does the check sit inside
     the row lock?
  2. Why is the job_id column still needed at all, now that driver lookups don't
     use it?

  Shall I push and open the PR?

  ⎿  Successfully loaded skill

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Here's the framing for the policy slice. This one isn't mechanical, so I'll
  wait for your OK, and there's one choice that's yours.

  Behaviour, as seen from outside:
  - A step whose attempt ended without success gets a new attempt when D4 allows
    it:
    - a failure the instrument marked retryable;
    - a hang on a device that's safe to repeat;
    - a lost read on a device that re-reads.
  - There are at most 3 attempts in total.
  - Otherwise the step ends failed, and the error says why it wasn't repeated,
    for example "instrument hung; outcome unknown; not repeated: incubator-1 is
    not safe to repeat".
  - The driver's command carries the real attempt number (K47).
  - A plate-reader hang is now repeated (K48).
  - Every attempt shows in the instrument's ledger as its own job (…/incubate/1,
    …/incubate/2).

  Checks that prove it:
  1. A table test of the decision function, covering each cause, the 3-attempt
     limit, a cancel, and a lost read that's already been re-read.
  2. End-to-end tests through the dispatch loop with the fake drivers:
     - a retryable incubator failure, then success: the run completes, each job
       ran once, and the command for attempt 2 says attempt: 2;
     - a failure every time: the step fails after exactly 3 attempts;
     - a lost read: it's re-read, the re-read decides the step, and the run
       completes.
  3. The existing suite under -race, acceptance, and a live run with
     INC_FAIL_PCT=100.

  Depends on: D4 and its amendments (the safe-repeat rule; only the plate reader
  is repeat-safe), D3, and D1 (cancel).

  How the decision is made safely: the decision is taken inside the step's row
  lock. The store gets a small helper that loads the step, decides, and applies
  the result in one transaction. The alternative, deciding from a snapshot and
  applying later, can act on stale state: for example, it could re-read after
  attempt 1's late upload had already completed the step. The lifecycle's guards
  would block most of those cases, but not all.

  Your choice: where the per-device settings live. D4 says they're "settings per
  device, not code".
  - (a) Recommended: environment variables on the executor in 
    docker-compose.yml: REPEAT_SAFE=plate-reader,
    REREAD_ON_DATA_LOSS=plate-reader, MAX_ATTEMPTS=3, with device-name prefixes
    as in producesData. You change them without a rebuild, and a /break run can
    flip one. About 20 extra lines for parsing.
  - (b) A table in policy.go. Simpler, but changing it means a rebuild, which
    goes against "settings, not code".

  Either way, tests set the policy directly.

  Size: about 110–140 non-test lines.

  OK to go ahead with (a)?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The D4 policy slice is built, tested and committed on branch
  slice/d4-retry-policy, in three commits: the policy (7668c56), the Codex fix
  (67022c8), and concerns (eaf5162). It's ready for your review.

  What it does: steps now get new attempts where D4 allows them, up to 3, and
  otherwise end failed with the reason they weren't repeated.

  How it works

  1. When a decision is needed: each dispatch tick (send.go:113) looks at every
     step that is ended (all attempts ended) or unresolved (for example, a lost
     read). It calls decideStep (store.go:26), which:
     - loads the step under its row lock;
     - asks the policy for one fact: a new attempt (Dispatched), a give-up with
       its reason (GaveUp), or nothing;
     - applies that fact (store.go:73), all in one transaction.
  2. The decision itself (Policy.next, policy.go:69) looks only at the latest
     attempt:

  | Latest attempt
     | Policy says                                                  |
  |-----------------------------------------------------------------------------
  ----|--------------------------------------------------------------|
  | Failed, and the instrument marked it Retryable
     | New attempt                                                  |
  | Failed, not marked retryable
     | Give up: "the instrument did not mark the failure retryable" |
  | Hung, on a device in REPEAT_SAFE
     | New attempt                                                  |
  | Hung, on any other device
     | Give up: "<device> is not safe to repeat"                    |
  | Read with lost data, on a device in REREAD_ON_DATA_LOSS
     | Re-read                                                      |
  | Read with lost data, on any other device
     | Nothing: it stays open (D3)                                  |
  | Anything else (in flight, completed, cancelled, given up, conflicting
  outcomes) | Nothing                                                      |
     - The limit: at 3 attempts the reason becomes "3 attempts made".
     - A give-up is only proposed once the step is ended. While an earlier lost
       read is still open, the step can't fail, so the policy returns nothing.
  3. The settings (policy.go:24-28): REPEAT_SAFE, REREAD_ON_DATA_LOSS and
     MAX_ATTEMPTS are set in docker-compose.yml.
     - The defaults are D4's: the plate reader, the plate reader, and 3.
     - An empty value means "no device"; a bad MAX_ATTEMPTS stops the executor
       at startup.
     - The executor logs the policy it's using.
  4. Commands carry the right attempt (send.go:211): the attempt number now
     comes from the job id instead of always being 1 (K47).

  Why this way

  - Deciding under the row lock: the decision is made on the step exactly as it
    is when the fact is applied. A late upload that completed the step, or a
    cancel, can't slip in between reading the step and acting on it.
  - The policy only proposes: the lifecycle's guards (next attempt number,
    nothing in flight, and so on) still apply on top.

  What could go wrong

  - Codex P2, fixed here as you chose (67022c8): the recheck for lost reads only
    looked at unresolved steps, so it stopped looking for attempt 1's upload
    while the re-read was in flight. Now it covers every step still running.
    Reproduced first by TestLostReadIsFoundWhileItsReReadIsStuck: the re-read is
    claimed but never started, and attempt 1's upload completes the run.
  - K50: the dispatch loop takes a row lock every 500ms for each ended or
    unresolved step, even when there's nothing to decide.
  - K51: retries happen at once, with no backoff.
  - K52: acceptance check 5 ("no step ran more than once") will fail on any run
    that retried.
  - K39 (narrowed): a lost read whose re-reads are also lost, up to the limit,
    stays open. Operator abandonStep is still deferred.

  Evidence (measured)

  - New tests:
    - TestNextAttempt: 15 cases.
    - TestPolicyFromEnv.
    - End to end:
      - a retryable failure is retried, and the command says attempt: 2;
      - the 3-attempt limit holds;
      - a hang is repeated only on a repeat-safe device;
      - a lost read is re-read, and the re-read supplies data_ref;
      - the stuck re-read case (Codex P2).

    All of them failed before the code existed.
  - Suites: go build, go vet and go test -race ./... all pass.
  - Mutation checks: 11 mutants, all of which compiled. 9 were caught at first.
    Of the other 2, one turned out to be a redundant check, which I removed; the
    other needed a test for an empty REPEAT_SAFE, and is now caught.
  - Acceptance: both workflows passed all 7 checks, before and after the fix.
  - Live, incubator failing every time: the ledger shows incubate/1–3 all
    failed. The record shows "instrument error during incubate; not repeated: 3
    attempts made".
  - Live, plate reader hanging every time: the ledger shows plate_read/1–3 all
    hung. The record shows "instrument hung; outcome unknown; not repeated: 3
    attempts made", and report stayed pending.
  - All devices are back on their defaults.

  Questions

     in every PCR run. What does the record say for that step, and why isn't it
     retried?
  2. A read loses its data and its re-read fails without the retryable flag. Why
     does the policy return nothing here, rather than giving up?

  Reproduce a driver crash after a claim but before the job starts. Make sure

⏺ Skill(slice)
  ⎿  Successfully loaded skill

⏺ First, the code that decides whether a claimed step can be safely resumed: how
  the driver answers a repeated command, and what K19 and K25 say:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Before writing any code I need your OK on the approach, because it departs
  from what D1 planned. Here's what I found.

  Why a claim-then-crash gets stuck today (inferred from the code):
  - The executor sends the command only while a step is dispatched. Once it's
    claimed, it stops sending and waits for started.
  - If the driver dies between the claim and inst.Run, the ledger has nothing,
    so no started ever comes, and the step stays running for ever.
  - After a restart, the driver's replay has nothing to report: the job isn't in
    its ledger, and it doesn't remember the claim.
  - The same stuck state happens when Run returns ErrBusy or a ledger write
    error after a granted claim (K19).

  What makes recovery safe: the instrument writes the ledger entry before any
  physical work (instrument.go: "Nothing physical happens unless the ledger has
  it first"). The driver's accept answers known for any job that is current in
  the driver, current on the instrument, or already in the ledger
  (adapter.go:96). So the driver accepts a job again only if it provably never
  started, and only an accepted job is claimed and run.

  Two ways to recover:
  - A. A reconcile query, as D1 planned (R2): a new subject lab.<device>.job.
    The driver answers from its ledger and its current job, and on "unknown" the
    executor resends.
  - B. Recommended: keep resending the same command while the step is claimed 
    with no evidence, every 3s, the same as after an accepted or known reply
    today.
    - The command already is the query: known means the job is in flight or in
      the ledger, so nothing more happens. accepted means the driver has no
      record of it, so it claims again; the executor answers go, because that
      attempt is already claimed; and it runs exactly once.
    - No new subject or driver code; about 5 lines in send.go.
    - It covers K19 too. It also works after an executor restart, because the
      resend is driven from Postgres.
    - Cost: normally a step is claimed for about 100ms before started arrives,
      so at most one extra command; for a long outage, one request every 3s per
      stuck step. A query (A) would cost the same messages plus a new protocol.

  I'd record B as an update to D1: R2 becomes "resend while claimed", not a
  query.

  Reproduction:
  1. Go test, written to fail first: a fake driver claims the job and then
     "crashes": it never runs it and stops serving. A fresh fake driver for the
     same device starts.
     - Today the run stays running.
     - After the fix, the new driver runs the job once and the run completes.
     - I'll also check that a claimed job still in flight gets known and isn't
       run twice.
  2. Live crash on the real stack, before and after the fix:
     - Stop liquid-handler-1, so the first step's command has no responder.
     - Hold the step's row lock in psql, then start the driver again. Its claim
       then blocks inside the executor.
     - docker kill the driver, then release the lock. The executor records
       Claimed and replies to a driver that no longer exists. That's exactly the
       state we're after: claimed, no ledger entry.
     - Then start the driver again and check:
       - before the fix: stuck running;
       - after the fix: the record is completed, and the ledger has exactly one
         execution.

  Not covered here (I'll log it as K54): a cancel that arrives after the claim
  of an attempt that never started. With B, the resend still runs that job. D1
  already says a claimed attempt can't be stopped, so this doesn't contradict
  anything, but it's for the CancelRun slice to handle.

  OK to go with B?

  Created PR #18 (ctrl+o to expand)
⏺ I reproduced a driver crash after a claim but before the job starts on the
  live stack. With the fix, the executor recovers on its own, keeps the same job
  id, and the physical step runs exactly once. PR #18 is open into dev:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/18

  The crash, reproduced live

  The moment between "claim granted" and "job started" is microseconds wide, so
  I made it wide enough to hit:
  1. Stop liquid-handler-1, so the first step's command has no one to answer it.
  2. Hold that step's row lock in psql, then start the driver. Its claim now
     waits inside the executor.
  3. docker compose kill the driver, then release the lock. The claim is
     recorded, and its reply goes to a driver that no longer exists.
  4. Start the driver again.

  Before the fix (run-42e468091aef): the step stayed claimed. The restarted
  driver received nothing and had nothing in its ledger, and the run was still
  running 20s later.

  After the fix:
  - That same stuck run recovered by itself once the new executor came up, and
    completed.
  - A fresh reproduction (run-1f9da810f3b8) also completed: fill_sample_plate/1
    started 1.4s after the driver came back.
  - In both runs, every job has exactly one execution in its ledger, and no
    attempt 2 exists anywhere.

  The fix

  One change in send.go: the dispatch loop keeps sending the command for a step
  that's claimed with no evidence, not only for dispatched ones. It's the same
  job id, every 3s, until started arrives.

  Why it can't run the operation twice

  The instrument writes its ledger entry before anything physical happens. The
  driver answers known for any job it has in flight, that its instrument is
  running, or that is already in its ledger. So:
  - If the job started, the resend gets known and nothing more happens.
  - If it never started, the driver accepts it and claims it again. The executor
    answers go, because that attempt was already claimed, and it runs once.

  Four tests pin this down; the first two failed before the fix:
  - TestClaimedJobIsResentAfterADriverCrash: a fake driver claims the job and
    then dies; the restarted one runs it once, still as one attempt.
  - TestResendWhileClaimedDoesNotRunTwice: a slow start spans several resends,
    and the job runs once.
  - TestRestartedDriverNeverRunsALedgerJobAgain: on the real driver and
    instrument, a job already in the ledger is known after a restart, and is
    never claimed or run.
  - TestRepeatDuringTheClaimIsKnown: a repeat that arrives while the claim is
    still being answered gets known.

  Mutation checks: all 4 mutants are caught. The last test was added because
  removing the driver's own in-flight check had survived the others.

  Other results

  - D1 updated: reconciliation is now the resend, not a separate query, with the
    reason in D1's change list. A query would only ask what the command already
    asks.
  - Suites: go build, go vet and go test -race ./... all pass, and acceptance
    passed all 7 checks on both workflows.
  - Codex: no findings; it couldn't run the tests in its sandbox, so its review
    was static only.

  Open concerns

  - K19 and K25 are done.
  - K54 (new): a cancel that arrives after the claim of a job that never started
    doesn't stop the resend. The CancelRun slice can answer such a re-claim
    with abort.
  - K55 (new): the no-duplicate guarantee assumes the instrument's ledger
    survives restarts, and that each instrument has only one driver process.
    I'll state that as an assumption in NOTES.md.

⏺ Skill(slice)
  ⎿  Successfully loaded skill

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Here's the framing for CancelRun. There's one choice that's yours (K42), so
  I'll wait for your OK before writing code.

  Behaviour, as seen from outside:
  - POST /runs/{id}/cancel stops the run from going any further: no new step is
    dispatched, and no new attempt is made.
  - Work that already started finishes, and its evidence is kept: the ledger's
    outcome, its times, and stored data.
  - The run ends once nothing is in flight. Its final status follows the K42
    rule below.

  What a cancel does to each step

  The whole cancel is one transaction over all of the run's steps, locked in
  position order (K12), so it can't be half-applied if the executor crashes
  partway.

  ┌───────────────┬──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │ The step when │                          │                             │
  │  the cancel   │       What happens       │         It ends as          │
  │    arrives    │                          │                             │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ pending       │ Nothing is ever sent     │ cancelled                   │
  │ (never sent)  │                          │                             │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ The claim, if it comes,  │                             │
  │ Sent, not     │ gets abort; exactly one  │ cancelled                   │
  │ claimed       │ of claim and cancel wins │                             │
  │               │  (D1)                    │                             │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ Not stopped, and its     │ Whatever its evidence says: │
  │ Claimed or    │ late facts are recorded  │  completed, failed, or      │
  │ running       │ (D1)                     │ running while that's still  │
  │               │                          │ unknown                     │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Waiting for a │                          │ failed, "…; not retried:    │
  │  retry        │ No new attempt           │ cancelled" (D4)             │
  │ decision      │                          │                             │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ A retry sent, │ That attempt is          │ failed, "…; not retried:    │
  │  not claimed  │ cancelled                │ cancelled" (attempt 1       │
  │               │                          │ really ran)                 │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ No re-read; it stays     │ running, then completed if  │
  │ A lost read   │ open until its upload    │ the upload lands            │
  │               │ lands (D3)               │                             │
  └───────────────┴──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  K54, a claim that never started:
  - The S7 resend keeps asking. When the restarted driver claims again, the
    executor answers abort and marks that attempt withdrawn, so the step ends
    cancelled.
  - Why abort is safe here: a driver asks for a claim only before it runs a job,
    and only for a job its ledger doesn't have. So any claim request proves
    that the driver asking hasn't started the job, and won't without go. That
    holds as long as each instrument has only one driver process (K55).

  Other cases:
  - A run not started yet: it becomes cancelled at once, with all its steps.
  - A run already finished: no change; the API returns it as it is.
  - An unknown run: 404.

  K42, the run's final status after a cancel: your choice.
  - (1) Recommended:
    - completed if every step completed (the cancel came too late to stop
      anything);
    - failed if some step failed on its own (the policy gave up, regardless of
      the cancel);
    - cancelled otherwise.
  - (2) cancelled whenever a cancel was requested and not every step completed,
    even when a step had already failed on its own.

  I recommend (1). "Cancelled" then means "the cancel is why this run stopped",
  and a real failure isn't hidden behind it. Steps keep their own truthful
  statuses either way.

  Checks:
  - Lifecycle tests for the withdrawn claim.
  - CancelRun tests: a pending run, a running run, a finished run, an unknown
    run.
  - End-to-end tests with fake drivers:
    a. cancel while a step is running: it completes and keeps its times, and the
       later steps are never sent;
    b. cancel while a retry is waiting: no attempt 2 runs;
    c. K54: claim, crash, cancel, restart: the restarted driver runs nothing,
       and the step ends cancelled.
  - A live run: cancel mid-run, then compare the ledgers.

  Not covered:
  - A cancelled run with an open lost read stays running until the upload lands,
    as D3 requires (the deferred abandonStep, K39).
  - A cancel doesn't stop work that has already started physically, by design
    (D1).

  Size: about 110 non-test lines. There's no schema change: the run-level cancel
  is worked out from the steps.

  Go with rule (1)?

⏺ Codex raised two findings. I checked both against the code (inferred):

  P2, a blocker: a cancel can mislabel a failure that was already final.
  - The race: a liquid-handler attempt fails, and that failure is never retried.
    If the cancel lands in the up to 500ms before the dispatch loop records
    GaveUp, the lifecycle sets the step failed with "…; not retried: cancelled",
    and the run ends cancelled.
  - Why it's false: the step was never going to be retried, so "not retried:
    cancelled" is untrue. And under your rule (1), the run should be failed.
  - Proposed fix: a cancel no longer decides an ended step by itself. The step
    stays ended until the dispatch loop decides, and the policy is asked even
    after a cancel:
    - if it would have repeated the attempt: "not retried: cancelled", which
      counts towards a cancelled run;
    - if it wouldn't have: the step's own reason (for example "not retryable"),
      which counts towards a failed run.
    - The cost is that a cancelled ended step stays running for up to 500ms.
      Some existing lifecycle test cases would change to expect ended.

  P1: a delayed claim could wrongly withdraw a job that's running. Recommend 
  deferring and documenting.
  - Codex's scenario:
    a. One executor holds the driver's first claim past the driver's timeout.
    b. A second executor answers the driver's retried claim with go, and the job
       starts.
    c. The cancel lands, and then the stale first claim marks the attempt
       withdrawn.
  - Why it can't happen in this stack: compose runs a single executor. NATS
    delivers one subscription's messages in order on one goroutine, and onClaim
    handles each one inline. So a stale claim is always processed before the
    driver's retry, never after it.
  - What it rests on: a single executor, the same kind of assumption as K55 (one
    driver process per instrument). With several executors sharing the claim
    queue it would be real.
  - Proposed handling: log it as K56, and state "one executor" as an assumption
    in D5 and NOTES.md.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Codex P2: a cancel that lands before the dispatch loop records GaveUp 
     turns a final non-retryable failure into "not retried: cancelled" and a 
     cancelled run. Fix it here? → Fix here (Recommended)
     · Codex P1: a delayed claim processed by a second executor after the 
     driver's retry got go could withdraw a running job. Our stack has one 
     executor, which processes claims in order. How to handle it? → Defer, 
     document (Recommended)

⏺ I opened PR #19 into dev:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/19. It covers K42
  and K54, plus the Codex P2 fix you chose. Your uncommitted .gitignore change
  and .DS_Store were left out of it.

  How it works

  CancelRun (services/executor/service.go) runs as one transaction:
  1. It locks the run row. An unknown run is an error; a finished run is
     returned unchanged.
  2. It locks every step in position order (K12) and applies Cancel{} to each.
  3. A run that hadn't started becomes cancelled right away.
  4. It commits, then pokes the loop.

  What the cancel does to each kind of step:
  - Not sent yet: never will be.
  - Sent but not claimed: the claim and the cancel take the same row lock, so
    exactly one of them wins (receive.go:88).
  - Claimed or running: not stopped, and its late facts are still recorded.

  Withdrawn claim (K54), in the Claimed case of lifecycle.go: a driver only asks
  for a claim before it runs a job, and only for a job its ledger doesn't have.
  So a second claim for a cancelled attempt with no evidence proves the job
  never started. The claim is withdrawn and the driver is told abort.

  The P2 fix: the cancel no longer decides a step whose attempts have all ended.
  Policy.next (policy.go:71) judges the attempt that ran:
  - Would have retried: "not retried: cancelled", and the run ends cancelled.
  - Final anyway (not retryable, not safe to repeat, attempts used up): the step
    keeps its own reason, and the run ends failed.
  - Every step completed: the run ends completed.

  That last rule is rule 1, in finish (send.go:146).

  Why this way

  Before the P2 fix, a cancel arriving after a final failure relabelled that
  failure "not retried: cancelled", and the run ended cancelled. The record
  blamed the cancel for something the instrument had already settled.

  What could go wrong

  - K56 (Codex P1, deferred as you chose): the withdrawal assumes one executor
    answers a driver's claims in order. With two executors, a stalled claim
    could be mistaken for a re-claim and withdraw an attempt that is actually
    running. It's written into D5 and goes in NOTES.md next to K55.
  - K39: a cancelled run whose lost read never shows up stays running.
  - K57: one mutant survived: judging the latest attempt instead of the one that
    ran. In practice both give the same answer, because a withdrawn attempt is
    always a retry the policy had already allowed. They differ only if the retry
    config changes mid-run (inferred).

  Evidence (all measured)

  - Tests: go test -race ./... passes, and 4 of the 5 deliberate code changes
    made the tests fail.
  - Acceptance: 7/7.
  - Live mid-run cancel, repeated after the fix:
    - The three steps the record calls completed are exactly the three
      executions in the ledgers.
    - The other four are cancelled and appear in no ledger.
  - Live K54 (run-57e726e482a0): the driver was killed holding a granted claim,
    then the run was cancelled and the driver restarted.
    - The re-claim was refused.
    - The step and the run are cancelled.
    - liquid-handler-1's ledger has no execution for the run.

  Questions to check your understanding

  1. An incubator step fails with a retryable error, and then the run is
     cancelled. Why does the step's error end in "not retried: cancelled", while
     a liquid-handler failure that wasn't marked retryable keeps its own
     reason? What does each do to the run's final status?
  2. Why is a second claim for the same job proof that the job never started?
     Which two assumptions (K55, K56) does that proof rest on?

  Created PR #20, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I opened PR #20 into dev:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/20. Tests, mutation
  checks and acceptance all pass, Codex found nothing, and the live checks
  against the ledgers hold.

  Housekeeping first. When I switched to dev, the working tree still held
  cancel-slice files, which looks like an incomplete branch switch. I checked
  that they matched the merged origin/dev. The tool wouldn't let me discard
  them, so I stashed them as stash@{0} ("partial switch leftovers…"). It
  contains nothing that isn't already in dev, so you can drop it with git stash 
  drop. Your .gitignore change and .DS_Store were not committed.

  How it works

  - Routing: main.go puts /graphql next to api.Routes(). api.go and the REST
    responses are unchanged, and acceptance passed 7/7.
  - One snapshot per read: each query runs in one read-only, repeatable-read
    transaction (Executor.snapshot, graphql.go). readRun reads the run row, then
    each step's REST columns and its lifecycle JSON in the same statement.
    attemptsOf turns the decoded lifecycle into the attempt list.
  - Each attempt reports:
    - its phase, from the existing Attempt.phase;
    - decided (Step.Decided == N);
    - the ledger outcome and times;
    - for reads, verified and every storage check, each marked as matching the
      ledger hash or not.
  - No new state: GraphQL derives nothing the lifecycle doesn't already say.
  - Mutations call CreateRun, StartRun and CancelRun, then read the record
    again.
  - Errors carry extensions.code. Internal detail goes only to the log, as in
    REST.

  Why this way

  - Same rows as REST: step fields come from the same columns REST reads, and
    saveStep writes those columns and the lifecycle in one UPDATE. So GraphQL
    can't contradict REST.
  - REST shape kept: K53 is fixed by adding attempts, not by changing REST.
  - What you chose: graph-gophers, so the 120-line schema in graphql.go is the
    design you defend, and polling instead of subscriptions. Both are recorded
    in D6.

  What could go wrong

  - K58, duplicate runs: a tool that retries createRun after a lost response
    creates a second run.
  - K59, REST can tear (pre-existing): REST GetRun reads in two statements
    without a snapshot, so for one poll it can show the run and its steps from
    different moments. The fix keeps the response shape unchanged.
  - K60, no pagination: runs has none.
  - K61, time precision: the step and its deciding attempt differ below 1µs,
    because Postgres stores microseconds while the lifecycle JSON keeps
    nanoseconds. Found live.

  Evidence (measured)

  - Live K53: with the plate reader set to always hang, GraphQL showed three
    HUNG attempts, each matching its own ledger execution. REST /timeline shows
    only the last 5s.
  - REST against GraphQL: on the acceptance run they agree on every step. The
    read's ledgerSha256 equals the plate reader's data_sha256.
  - Tests: three new tests pass. 9 of 9 deliberately broken versions of the
    resolvers made a test fail. Three of them initially went unnoticed, so I
    tightened the tests: decided is false on a read that completed with
    mismatched data, an unknown run returns null with no error, and a completed
    attempt is not retryable.
  - One wrong guess along the way: I expected a mismatched storage object to
    show as STORING. The lifecycle says UNRESOLVED, which is correct, and the
    test now expects that.

  Questions to check your understanding

  1. Why does a step's status come from the stored column, while an attempt's
     phase is computed from the lifecycle on each read? What would it take for
     the two to disagree?
  2. A scientist sees read_plate as COMPLETED, with verified: true and a later
     check showing matches: false. What happened (D2), and why doesn't the step
     go back to failed?

  Created PR #21, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I ran the final chaos pass, C4, over 26 runs. Every run record matched the
  instrument ledgers and S3, so there was nothing to fix. The experiment is
  logged in docs/chaos-log.md and in PR #21:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/21.

  How runs were checked. I wrote scripts/check-run.py. For each run it checks
  that:
  - every step and attempt matches its ledger entry (outcome and times);
  - every ledger execution appears as an attempt, and no job ran twice;
  - every completed read's object in S3 has the ledger's sha256.

  I fed it doctored ledgers to make sure it catches problems, and it flagged all
  six kinds I tried. It also refuses to pass a run when it can't read storage.

  What happened (measured)

  Fault: Executor killed mid-step / mid-read
  Predicted: completed
  Observed: completed, 7 executions
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Plate reader killed mid-read
  Predicted: attempt 1 hung, attempt 2 completes
  Observed: as predicted; ledger says "instrument restarted during the job",
  data
  from /2.csv with a matching hash
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Liquid handler killed mid-combine
  Predicted: combine failed, not safe to repeat
  Observed: as predicted; read_plate never sent
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Incubator killed while idle
  Predicted: completed late
  Observed: completed; the executor re-sent the command every second until the
  driver was back
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Executor cut off 8s
  Predicted: completed, a few seconds late
  Observed: completed, but about 23s late (see below)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Plate reader cut off while its read ends
  Predicted: completed
  Observed: completed; upload and events went through after reconnecting
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Storage down 15s
  Predicted: completed about 5s after storage returns
  Observed: completed, but 17s after (see below)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Random faults, seeds 7 and 11
  Predicted: all completed
  Observed: all completed
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fault: Random faults + instrument faults, seeds 13 and 29
  Predicted: seed 13 retries then completes; seed 29 fails on warm_reagent_plate
  Observed: both as predicted

  What I got wrong

  - Executor partition (K62, new concern):
    - Events handed to the executor just as it was cut off are redelivered only
      after the 30s acknowledgement timeout (ackWait). I confirmed this by
      repeating the run while polling NATS: two events stayed unacknowledged for
      exactly 30s.
    - Meanwhile the record said running for steps that had already ended. That
      is behind, not false, so I logged it rather than changed code.
  - Storage outage:
    - SeaweedFS takes about 16s to serve again after any outage, even a 2s one.
    - Our upload retries every 0.5s and succeeded on the first retry once
      storage answered. The delay is storage's, not ours.
  - First instrument-fault prediction:
    - I didn't work out the fault rolls beforehand. Faults depend only on seed,
      device, step and attempt, so seed 3 gave every run the same three
      plate-reader hangs.
    - The record was still true. I then picked seeds 13 and 29 from a computed
      table so the runs would exercise retries and a failure.

  Defaults and acceptance

  - Settings: every *_PCT is back to 0, CHAOS_SEED=0, HANG_DURATION=30s.
  - Acceptance: 7/7 on PCR Amplification and 7/7 on Triple Assay.
  - Not re-run: go test (no Go code changed in this pass).
  - Not committed: your .gitignore change and .DS_Store.

  Questions to check your understanding

  1. Why did an 8s executor partition delay the run by about 30s? What would
     lowering ackWait cost, and why is a duplicate delivery harmless here?
  2. When a driver crashes mid-job, the ledger's finished_at is the restart
     time, not when the work stopped. Why is that still the right time for the
     record to show?

⏺ I drafted NOTES.md from the README, decisions, concerns and chaos log, and
  opened it as PR #23:
  https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/23

  What's in each section:
  1. Topology: a diagram; a table of the four channels with who replies and who
     acknowledges; one step from dispatch to verify; how each kind of crash is
     recovered; where each piece of state lives. The subject names, timings and
     key format come from the code.
  2. Why:
     - The main design choice (D1) is backed by the NATS partition experiment
       (C1), which showed that a timeout means "unknown".
     - D2 to D5 each say what was chosen, what was rejected, and why.
     - Where something was measured, it says so. For example, SeaweedFS ignores
       If-None-Match, and the faults depend only on seed, device, step and
       attempt.
  3. GraphQL: a condensed schema and why it's shaped that way. It reads the same
     rows as REST, shows every attempt (K53) and the stored-data evidence (K1),
     uses polling instead of subscriptions, and uses graph-gophers.
  4. Breaking it: a table of the four experiments, C1 to C4, with commands and
     seeds. It says where my predictions were wrong and what changed because of
     each. It also covers the checker script, how the checker itself was tested,
     and the race and mutation checks.
  5. Still not true or not proven, in four groups:
     - Deliberate boundaries: no stopping work in progress, no operator tools
       (K6/K39).
     - Assumptions: K55, K56, K16, a single NATS server.
     - Known gaps: K5, K59, K58, K62, K51, K61.
     - Not proven: K8, C2 not re-run live, concurrent runs on one device, scale,
       K38.

  How it's set up
  - docs/forai/ holds the originals, unchanged apart from a pointer at the top
    of each. They stay the working log, so /decide, /slice, /break and CLAUDE.md
    still write there.

    forengineer/. If the two versions disagree, forai/ is the one to trust.

    index. Code comments also point to forengineer/. CLAUDE.md and the skills
    point to forai/.

  What the rewrite changes

    sections are worked into the text, so a reader doesn't have to apply them in
    their head. The history stays in forai/.
  - decisions.md:
    - Terms like ledger, attempt, job id, claim and phase are defined once at
      the top.
    - Each decision follows the same order: the problem, the choice, how it
      works, the rejected alternatives (as a table), what it depends on, and its
      limits.
    - D1 also has a step-by-step diagram, a table of the message channels and a
      table of step phases.
    - Internal codes like S7 and R1 are replaced by what they did.
  - concerns.md: the 62-row table is now grouped by status, then by theme: runs
    that can stay running for ever, views that lag, faults and clients,
    configuration, operations, and scale. It opens with the five concerns that
    matter most, and resolved items are one line each with their fix.
  - chaos-log.md: a summary table first, then each experiment as why, what we
    did, what we expected, what happened, and what we learned. C3 and C4 show
    expected against observed side by side. Run ids and log lines stay in
    forai/.

❯ Create one polished, interactive HTML explainer for the whole project, under  
  docs/html/.                                                                   
                                                                                
  The goal is to help an engineer unfamiliar with this project understand the   
  architecture, key design decisions, and failure recovery in about 5 minutes.  
  It should also be useful when walking through the project in an interview.    
                                                                                
  Use NOTES.md, docs/forengineer/decisions.md, and the actual code as sources.  
  Reuse useful material from the existing HTML explainers, but don't duplicate  
  them.                                                                         
                                                                                
  Design it around three sections:                                              
                                                                                
  Explore the architecture: A clean, clickable topology showing the executor,   
  Postgres, Core NATS, JetStream, drivers, instruments and their ledgers, and   
  S3. Clicking a component explains what it owns and why it's needed. Visually  
  distinguish commands, claims, durable events, and data uploads.               
                                                                                
  Follow a run: An interactive, step-by-step walkthrough showing dispatch →     
  claim → instrument execution → durable events → S3 verification → completion. 
  Highlight the active components and messages at each step. Show what the API  
  knows and what evidence supports it.                                          
                                                                                
  What happens when things go wrong: Let the reader select realistic scenarios  
  such as a driver crash before execution, a crash during execution, a safe     
  retry after a hang, a lost read with a late upload, and cancellation. Show    
  how the system recovers, what gets retried, and why the run record doesn't    
  invent an outcome.                                                            
                                                                                
  Make the most important decisions easy to understand: why Core NATS for       
  commands, JetStream for events, claim-before-run, stable JobIDs, the          
  instrument ledger, S3 hash verification, and safe retry rules.                
                                                                                
  Prioritize clarity over visual effects. Use simple English, concise           
  explanations, progressive disclosure, clear arrows, consistent colors, and a  
  readable layout. Include a small legend and make it obvious where each piece  
  of state lives.                                                               
                                                                                
  Build it as a self-contained HTML file with CSS and vanilla JavaScript. No    
  external dependencies, servers, or build steps. Make it responsive and        
  keyboard-accessible, with working Next, Back, and Reset controls.             
                                                                                
  Avoid large walls of text, cluttered diagrams, meaningless animations, or     
  features that don't help explain the system.                                  
                                                                                
  Verify every explanation and scenario against the implementation. Don't imply 
  exactly-once message delivery or guaranteed recovery where we don't have it.  
  Explicitly distinguish known outcomes from uncertain ones.                    
                                                                                
  Test all interactive controls and check the layout at desktop and mobile      
  sizes.                                                                        
                                                                                
  Keep the existing NOTES.md as the main submission document. This HTML is a    
  visual companion, not a replacement.                                          
                                                                                
  Show me the result and briefly explain the design choices before making       
  further documentation changes.

⏺ The explainer is ready and open in your browser:
  docs/html/system-overview.html, in PR #25
  (https://github.com/buildsmall-dev/automata-challenge/pull/25). It's stacked
  on #24, and I haven't changed any other docs.

  What's in it
  1. Explore the architecture: a clickable diagram of the system. Clicking a
     part shows what it owns, why it's needed, and what happens when it fails.
     Clicking a line type in the legend highlights that kind of message. A table
     below shows where each piece of state lives and whether it survives a
     restart.
  2. Follow a run: one plate read in 8 steps, from dispatch to verified
     completion. Each step shows what the API says next to the evidence (ledger
     and S3), and marks what changed since the last step.
  3. What happens when things go wrong: six scenarios, each ending with a short
     summary:
     - driver crashes before the job starts;
     - driver crashes during a job;
     - instrument hangs, with a switch between the plate reader (retried) and
       the liquid handler (fails);
     - lost read, then a late upload;
     - cancel mid-run;
     - executor crashes or is cut off.
  4. The decisions: the seven you listed, as cards that open on click. Each
     links to the scenario that shows it. Below them is a box on what the design
     does not promise.

  Design choices
  - One diagram everywhere. The same diagram, in the same position, is used in
    every section, so you learn the layout once. Each section only highlights
    parts of it.
  - Every message type differs in colour and line style. Commands are blue solid
    lines, claims purple dotted, durable events thick green, and data uploads
    orange dashed. They can be told apart without colour too.
  - Each step is labelled Known, Unknown or Intent. This makes it visible where
    the record says running because the outcome really is open, rather than
    making something up.
  - Not overselling it. The page says delivery can repeat ("at least once"),
    with job ids and idempotent apply making repeats harmless. It says a run can
    stay running for ever (K6, K39), and that after a network cut the record
    can lag by up to 30s (K62). Each measured claim names its experiment.
  - Short text, details on demand. Each panel has 2–3 bullets and one "why"
    line, and the decision cards open only when clicked. There are no
    animations; highlighting and labels on the arrows show the flow.

  Checked against the code. I traced each scenario through the executor and
  driver code, and corrected two things in my own draft:
  - The liquid-handler hang now uses the step that experiment C3a actually
    measured.
  - The cancel race now describes an order of events that can really happen.

  Tested in headless Chrome at 1280, 768 and 375px, and in dark mode:
  - Every component, legend entry and step control works, including the arrow
    keys.
  - Every scenario and both device options run through to the end.
  - Every decision card and "See it" link works.
  - No console errors, and no sideways page scrolling at any width.

  Testing caught two bugs, now fixed: the device switch stayed visible after
  leaving the hang scenario, and the diagram text was too small on desktop. On
  phones the diagram switches to a taller layout, and Back / Next / Reset stay
  pinned to the bottom of the screen.

  For you to decide:
  - Should the explainer be linked from NOTES.md and docs/README.md? I held off,
    since you asked to see it before any further doc changes.
  - The example ids and times (run-3c9e, 12:00:08) are made up, and the page
    says so. I can swap in a real run from C4 if you'd rather.

       45 +### The main idea                                             
       46 +                                                              
       47 +One rule shaped the design: **durable facts, short-lived inten
          +tions, and Postgres decides.**                                
       48 +                                                              
       49 +- **Intentions** ("run this job") travel as plain NATS request
          +s. They aren't stored anywhere                                
       50 +  on the way. If one is lost it is simply sent again. A comman
          +d must never sit in a queue                                   
       51 +  and run later, after a cancel, or after someone has moved th
          +e plate.                                                      
       52 +- **Facts** ("this job started", "it finished at 12:00:10") tr
          +avel through a JetStream                                      
       53 +  stream. They are kept until the executor has saved them, so 
          +they survive the executor                                     
       54 +  being down.                                                 
       55 +- **Postgres decides.** The executor applies every fact and ev
          +ery decision in one database                                  
       56 +  transaction. Postgres is the only place that says what shoul
          +d happen next.

          -t/reply, queue group `executor` | driver → executor | `go` or 
          -`abort`. No reply if the database fails |                     
       60 -| 3 | `lab.<device>.evt.<kind>`, kind = `started`, `finished`,
          - `stored` or `data_lost` | JetStream stream `LAB_EVENTS` (file
          - storage), `Nats-Msg-Id` = `<job_id>:<kind>` | driver → execut
          -or | The driver retries until it gets a PubAck. The executor a
          -cks only after its database commit |                          
       61 -| 4 | `runs/<run>/<step>/<attempt>.csv` | S3 PUT, then a `stor
          -ed` event | driver → storage | The executor reads the object b
          -ack and hashes it |                                           
       92 +| 1 | `lab.<device>.cmd.run` | NATS request | executor → drive
          +r | `accepted`, `busy` or `known` (the driver already has this
          + job). No reply means *unknown* |                             
       93 +| 2 | `lab.executor.claim` | NATS request, queue group `execut
          +or` | driver → executor | `go` or `abort`. No reply if the dat
          +abase fails |                                                 
       94 +| 3 | `lab.<device>.evt.<kind>`: `started`, `finished`, `store
          +d`, `data_lost` | JetStream stream `LAB_EVENTS` | driver → exe
          +cutor | The driver retries until JetStream confirms. The execu
          +tor acks only after its database commit |                     
       95 +| 4 | `runs/<run>/<step>/<attempt>.csv` | S3 PUT | driver → st
          +orage | The executor reads the object back and hashes it |    
       96  
       63 -`job_id` is `<run>/<step>/<attempt>`. A job is one attempt at 
          -one step, and it is the unit of                               
       64 -idempotency everywhere.                                       
       97 +Every job has an id `<run>/<step>/<attempt>`, for example `run
          +-3c9e/read_plate/1`. It stays                                 
       98 +the same however many times a message about it is sent, and it
          + is the unit of idempotency                                   
       99 +throughout: the driver answers `known` to a repeated command, 
          +JetStream drops a repeated                                    
      100 +event by its message id (`<job id>:<kind>`), and the storage k
          +ey comes from it.                                             
      101  
       66 -### One step, in order                                        
      102 +### A normal run, step by step                                
      103  
       68 -1. **Dispatch.** The dispatch loop picks the steps `NextSteps`
          - says are ready. It records a                                 
       69 -   `Dispatched` fact, then sends the command.                 
       70 -   - **`busy` or no answer:** asks again after 500ms.         
       71 -   - **`accepted` or `known`:** asks again after 3s, until the
          -re is evidence the job started.                               
       72 -   - **A timeout:** means *unknown*. The command is resent wit
          -h the same `job_id`, never                                    
       73 -     as a new attempt.                                        
       74 -2. **Claim.** Before `inst.Run`, the driver asks `lab.executor
          -.claim`.                                                      
       75 -   - **The executor's side:** it applies `Claimed` under the s
          -tep's row lock, the same lock a                               
       76 -     cancel takes, so exactly one of claim and cancel wins.   
       77 -   - **`go`:** the driver runs the job.                       
       78 -   - **`abort` or no answer:** the job doesn't run. On no answ
          -er the driver asks again.                                     
       79 -3. **Run and report.** The driver reads how the job went from 
          -the **ledger**, not from `Run`'s                              
       80 -   return value. It publishes `started` and `finished` with th
          -e ledger's times and outcome,                                 
       81 -   and forwards the instrument's `retryable` flag on a failure
          -.                                                             
       82 -4. **Data.** For a read, the driver uploads the bytes, retryin
          -g until storage takes them. It                                
       83 -   keeps the device busy meanwhile, because the bytes exist on
          -ly in its memory. Then it                                     
       84 -   publishes `stored`.                                        
       85 -5. **Verify.** The executor does not take `stored` on trust. I
          -t reads the object at the key it                              
       86 -   derives itself and hashes it.                              
       87 -   - **Hash matches the ledger's sha256:** the read is complet
          -ed, and only then does it get a                               
       88 -     `data_ref`.                                              
       89 -6. **Apply.** Every event becomes one fact, applied idempotent
          -ly in one transaction. Then the                               
       90 -   event is acked. Messages that can't apply are handled like 
          -this:                                                         
       91 -   - **Malformed, or from a device that doesn't match its subj
          -ect:** Term'd.                                                
       92 -   - **For a job no step has:** acked and logged.             
       93 -   - **Database error:** Nak'd, and retried after 1s.         
       94 -   - **An object not readable yet:** Nak'd, and retried after 
          -2s.                                                           
      104 +Take `read_plate`, the last step of PCR Amplification, on `pla
          +te-reader-1`:                                                 
      105  
       96 -### Recovery paths                                            
      106 +1. **Dispatch.** The dispatch loop runs every 500ms, and at on
          +ce when a new fact arrives. It                                
      107 +   asks Postgres which steps are ready. `combine` has complete
          +d, so `read_plate` is ready.                                  
      108 +   The executor records attempt 1 in Postgres *first*, then se
          +nds the command.                                              
      109 +2. **Command.** The driver answers `accepted`. The executor ke
          +eps sending the same command                                  
      110 +   until there is evidence the job started: after 500ms on `bu
          +sy` or no answer, and every 3s                                
      111 +   on `accepted` or `known`.                                  
      112 +3. **Claim.** Just before touching the instrument, the driver 
          +asks the executor for                                         
      113 +   permission. The executor records the claim under the step's
          + row lock and answers `go`.                                   
      114 +4. **Run.** The instrument writes the job to its ledger before
          + doing anything physical. The                                 
      115 +   driver reads the ledger every 50ms and publishes `started`,
          + with the ledger's time.                                      
      116 +5. **Finish.** The ledger says `completed`, with the sha256 of
          + the data. The driver publishes                               
      117 +   `finished`, with the outcome, both times and the hash.     
      118 +6. **Upload.** The driver uploads the bytes to `runs/run-3c9e/
          +read_plate/1.csv`, retrying                                   
      119 +   until storage accepts them. Then it publishes `stored`.    
      120 +7. **Verify.** The executor doesn't trust `stored`. It reads t
          +he object from the key it                                     
      121 +   computes itself and hashes it. The hash matches the ledger'
          +s, so the step is `completed`                                 
      122 +   and gets its `data_ref`.                                   
      123 +8. **Finish the run.** No step is in flight and every step com
          +pleted, so the run is                                         
      124 +   `completed`. Its `finished_at` is the last ledger time, not
          + the moment the executor                                      
      125 +   noticed.                                                   
      126  
       98 -- **Driver restart (R1):** before taking commands, the driver 
          -replays its whole ledger as                                   
       99 -  `started` and `finished` events.                            
      100 -  - **A job cut off by the restart:** the instrument closes it
          - as `hung` (`instrument.go:341`).                             
      101 -  - **A completed read:** the driver asks storage. Present mea
          -ns it publishes `stored`.                                     
      102 -    Absent after an upload grace (2 × the storage timeout) mea
          -ns it publishes `data_lost`.                                  
      103 -    No answer means it asks again.                            
      104 -- **Claimed, but no evidence (S7):** the same command is resen
          -t every 3s. The command itself                                
      105 -  asks the driver what it knows:                              
      106 -  - **`known`:** the job is in flight or in the ledger, so it 
          -ran or is running.                                            
      107 -  - **`accepted`:** the driver has no record of the job, so it
          - never started. It runs once.                                 
      108 -  - **No answer:** the outcome stays unknown, and the command 
          -is asked again.                                               
      109 -- **Executor restart:** all intent is in Postgres and every ev
          -ent waits in the stream.                                      
      110 -  Nothing in executor memory needs recovering.                
      111 -- **Lost read (D3):** every 10s the executor searches storage 
          -for the data of reads                                         
      112 -  reported lost.

          -or intent.** `status`, `phase`, the times, `error` and `data_r
          -ef` are derived from the lifecycle and written in the same `UP
          -DATE` |

      137 +The executor tracks finer phases than the API shows. `dispatch
          +ed`, `claimed`, `storing`,                                    
      138 +`unresolved` and others are visible in GraphQL (§3).          
      139  
      125 -**Lifecycle phases:**                                         
      126 -- `pending` and `dispatched` show as API `pending`.           
      127 -- `retrying`, `claimed`, `running`, `storing`, `unresolved` an
          -d `ended` show as API `running`.                              
      128 -- `completed`, `failed` and `cancelled` are terminal.         
      140 +Every event is applied in one transaction under the step's row
          + lock, then acked. An event                                   
      141 +that can't be applied is handled by kind:                     
      142  
      130 -A run finishes only when nothing is in flight. Its `finished_a
          -t` is the last ledger time, not                               
      131 -the time the executor noticed.                                
      143 +- **Malformed, or from a device that doesn't match its subject
          +:** rejected for good.                                        
      144 +- **A database error:** redelivered after 1s.                 
      145 +- **A `stored` object that can't be read yet:** redelivered af
          +ter 2s.                                                       
      146 +- **For a job no step has:** acked and logged.                
      147  
      148 +### When something goes wrong                                 
      149 +                                                              
      150 +Each row is explained in §2, and most were tested in §4.      
      151 +                                                              
      152 +| What fails | What happens | What the record says meanwhile |
          + Evidence |                                                   
      153 +| --- | --- | --- | --- |                                     
      154 +| A command is lost, or gets no reply | Sent again with the sa
          +me job id | Unchanged | measured (C4e) |                      
      155 +| The driver dies after its claim, before starting | Resent ev
          +ery 3s. The restarted driver has no record of the job, so it a
          +ccepts it, claims it again, gets `go`, and runs it once | `run
          +ning`, no start time | measured (resend PR #18) |             
      156 +| The driver dies mid-job | On restart the instrument marks th
          +e job `hung`, ending at the restart. Replay reports it. The pl
          +ate reader repeats it; the liquid handler doesn't | `running`,
          + then the retry's outcome, or `failed` with the reason | measu
          +red (C4c, C4d) |                                              
      157 +| The instrument hangs | After 30s the ledger says `hung`. The
          +n as above | `running`, then as above | measured (C3) |       
      158 +| The driver dies before uploading a read | Replay finds no ob
          +ject, and reports `data_lost`. The read stays open, is read ag
          +ain, and storage is checked every 10s | `running`, with the re
          +ason in `error` | measured (C2), re-read by unit tests |      
      159 +| The executor is down or cut off | Events wait in JetStream. 
          +A driver asking for a claim gets no answer, so nothing starts.
          + On return it resumes from Postgres | API unavailable, then co
          +rrect but late | measured (C4a, C4b, C4f) |                   
      160 +| Storage is down | The driver retries the upload, and keeps t
          +he plate reader busy | `running` until the data is verified | 
          +measured (C4h) |
